## Early in the morning at the break of dawn, they used to sleep until the afternoon

JPMorgan Chase Bank NA

This week<sup>’</sup>s data releases reinforce our views that global growth will remain resilient and core inflation sticky at an elevated level for major central banks. This news is likely to temper the conviction among central banks that pivoted toward an easing bias late last year, particularly relative to confidence expressed by Chair Powell at his January press conference. Caution and data dependence should be the watch words next week from Chair Powell<sup>’</sup>s testimony and the ECB meeting. Both are likely to affirm their views that inflation will move lower and open a window for easing later this year, but to eschew guidance on the timing and magnitude of the rate cuts ahead. Aligned with this view, the Fed<sup>’</sup>s recent Monetary Policy Report released ahead of the upcoming testimony indicated that “[t]he Committee does not expect it will be appropriate to reduce the target range until it has gained greater confidence that inflation is moving sustainably toward 2 percent.<sup>”</sup>

Activity indicators are consistent with a moderation in GDP growth from its strong 2H23 pace, but have been firmer than we expected. As a result, our global growth forecasts are rising alongside the current-quarter data flow—continuing the general pattern of recent quarters (Figure 1). On the heels of an upward move this week to a 2.25%ar forecast for 1Q US growth, we now project above-potential 2.6%ar global growth this quarter. Together with solid US performance, the global economy is turning into the new year with greater balance, a point underscored by the February manufacturing PMI survey (Figure 2). The output survey took a significant step up in the first two months of the year and now aligns with our forecast for roughly 2%ar factory output growth in 1H24. In the details, it is encouraging to see a rebound in the orders/inventory ratio and the capital goods PMI, which lifts our CapexNow tracking

for global equipment spending. If we are right, next week<sup>’</sup>s February data should further bolster confidence that the global expansion is moving forward broadly. We expect a rise in the global all-industry PMI, led by Western Europe, and a strong US payroll increase (200k).

As we turned into the new year, we maintained conviction that a sharp 2H23 slide in global core goods price inflation was ending while a material disinflation in services prices was unlikely this year. A wide array of global indicators pointed to fading goods deflation, whereas macroeconomic fundamentals—tight labor markets, elevated labor cost gains, and still-solid services demand—gave us confidence that services prices would remain sticky. Last quarter<sup>’</sup>s sharp deceleration in US PCE and Euro area HICP service price inflation challenged this view. Having faded these declines as inconsistent with fundamentals, it is encouraging to see the rebound in service price inflation in this week<sup>’</sup>s reports (Figure 3). A sharp rise in January US “supercore<sup>”</sup> PCE suggests the underlying pace of service price inflation remains at about a 4%ar. For the Euro area, a surprisingly strong increase in flash service prices last month points to a 3.7%ar rise in core service prices this quarter. This mix of an end to goods price deflation and still elevated service price gains is on track to deliver core inflation at roughly 3%ar in both economies in 1H24.

Figure 3: Core services inflation %3m, saar; Euro area thru Feb.
![](images/bfd000a934f94b6bfc1cba7cf3f064b61e52454a8e208e5651496f8a2b0d2fd1.jpg)

In a note this week on central bank reaction functions, we emphasized that modest correlated surprises in economic outcomes can produce large differences in cumulative action. Just as a recession threat will likely lever a large disinflationary DM easing, the alignment of modest upside surprises to growth, wage and price inflation will likely be magnified through central bankers<sup>’</sup> assessments of risk and their (unobservable) estimates of neutral rates and slack. Against this backdrop, our top-down views of resilience and sticky inflation appear inconsistent with the roughly 150bp easing currently priced for Fed and the ECB over 2024-25.

## ECB in watching mode

“Sources<sup>”</sup> suggest that progress is being made on the ECB<sup>’</sup>s operating framework review, with an announcement of the results possible later this month. We do not expect any substantive changes are imminent, and next week<sup>’</sup>s meeting will focus on the new staff forecasts and ECB communication. Staff forecasts are likely to reduce 2024 growth modestly while incorporating expectations of significantly lower energy prices. These shifts are not expected to feed through to wages or core HICP inflation, which is expected to move gradually lower. Focus will center on whether firmer inflation in the first two months of the year will temper the Governing Council<sup>’</sup>s statement that the “declining trend in underlying inflation has continued.<sup>”</sup> A shift of this type would likely close the door on easing prospects before midyear.

## Looking for a modest UK tax cut

We estimated there would be fiscal headroom for the Chancellor to use next week<sup>’</sup>s Budget to deliver a 1%-pt cut in the basic rate of income tax. While recent media briefings suggest the headroom is less, interest rates are also lower than assumed at last year<sup>’</sup>s Autumn Statement. Given this flexibility we stick with our original estimate. The Chancellor has a number of other options to deliver income tax cuts, including choosing a cheaper employee national insurance tax reduction, or pledging to cut spending by more after this year<sup>’</sup>s election; our best guess is it won<sup>’</sup>t come to that unless the Chancellor opts for a larger tax cut than we describe above.

## EMAX CBs hold on financial stability worry

Inflation is set to remain above central bank targets across the DM in 1H24, but in many EM economies it is declining below target (Figure 4). In EM Asia, real policy rates never became elevated, due to the later pandemic reopening across the region. This more limited buffer against shifts in global financial conditions is precluding many central banks from the easing already underway elsewhere in EM. Except in India and the Philippines, financial stability is a statutory mandate for central banks in the region, with alternative tools that limit the focus on policy rate adjustment. Where we expect cautious easing cycles—Korea and Thailand—central banks have concerns about elevated household debt levels.

## China to deliver more fiscal ease

Policy supports in China are building as fiscal initiatives have been accompanied by a large ongoing injection of liquidity.

We believe more will be needed to reach the 2024 growth target of “around 5%<sup>”</sup> and look to next week<sup>’</sup>s National People<sup>’</sup>s Congress (NPC) for signs of what comes next. On the fiscal side, we expect the central government deficit target to be raised to 3.8% of GDP (from last year<sup>’</sup>s 3.4%). Accounting for new spending announced in October, the implied 2024 deficit would be 4.2% of GDP. The impact of this impulse on the economy will likely be tempered as local government sources of funding—land sales and LGFV borrowing— remain constrained. A key concern about policy support to date has been its bias toward increasing productive capacity rather than consumer demand. This has resulted in a buildup of inventories pushing down domestic and export prices. Any sign of a shift in focus would be constructive. Finally, the economic impact of macro policy support has waned, as it is no longer accompanied by supporting countercyclical regulatory changes. We do not expect change on this front.

## BoJ’s dilemma favors B/S move before hike

This week<sup>’</sup>s Japan data showed a sharp decline in production and weak consumer spending continuing into January. While the upcoming spring wage negotiations will likely point to higher growth than last year, we pushed back the consumption recovery until 2Q when wage increases will actually be paid. Thus we revise down 1Q GDP-based real consumption growth to 0.2%ar (from 0.8%). Given the large gap between strong market momentum and softening economic activity, we think BoJ changes in its asset purchase programs will take precedence over rate hikes at the start of policy normalization. We also think rate normalization will await confirmation of increases in the spring wage negotiations and a demand recovery that we see happening around mid-year.

## Türkiye: Strong growth despite rate hikes

GDP growth in Türkiye accelerated to a 3.9%ar in 4Q23, from 1.3% the quarter prior. This improvement was mainly driven by stronger household consumption, which rose 15.4%ar versus -6.6% in 3Q23. For the year as a whole GDP expanded 4.5%, which suggests an overheating economy with a 1.5% output gap in 2023. Activity is likely to be even stronger this quarter due to wage hikes and fiscal stimulus, and leading indicators signal a pickup in consumer spending. We now see upside risks to our GDP forecast of 2.7% this year, as current monetary and fiscal policy may not be tight enough to cool household consumption. We will re-assess our growth and inflation forecasts after the March municipal elections, should monetary or fiscal policies shift. With Türkiye<sup>’</sup>s economy still running above potential, we see a greater chance for the next move to be a hike than a cut.

## Bank of Israel to tread carefully

Economic conditions in Israel support further, potentially substantial, monetary easing. GDP growth plunged 19.4%ar in 4Q23 while inflation momentum slowed below target recently, running at an annualized three-month rate of 1.2% (and just 0.4% for core). Yet the BoI surprised markets and kept rates unchanged at 4.5% this week. It also communicated a preference to move gradually given still elevated uncertainty about the impact of the war on the economy. Inflation risks could be skewed higher—inflation expectations have risen, some sectors face supply constraints (including disruptions to maritime trade and labor shortages in construction and agriculture), and fiscal policy is turning more expansionary. Even with these risks, we believe the case for easing remains strong. Indeed, the BoI signaled rate cuts are coming once it gains confidence inflation will remain low. We now assume a more gradual easing trajectory in Israel, but with the policy rate still at 3.75% by year-end. Expansionary fiscal policy is the main constraint for more substantial monetary easing.

## Nigeria: battling tough policy choices

A sharp 70% currency depreciation has pressured Nigeria<sup>’</sup>s policymakers. While broad-ranging policy and FX reforms have begun after last year<sup>’</sup>s elections, large economic imbalances had built up after years of capital account restrictions, fiscal financing, and less orthodox FX policies—all introduced after the 2015 collapse in oil prices. This week a step in the right direction, and a strong show of intent, came in the form of a larger-than-expected 400bp hike by the central bank. But the broader macroeconomic policy stance remains loose, and to sustainably ease inflation and currency pressures we think further monetary tightening is needed (both rate hikes and liquidity management), as well as steps to reduce fiscal dominance (including tighter fiscal policy). We look for the CBN to hike another 200bp hike at its March meeting.

## Signals of 1Q LatAm growth recovery

For the better part of the past three years, Latin America has grown at a solid pace—and faster than initially anticipated. We look for this pattern to continue into the current quarter. Data this week confirmed a stall in Brazil<sup>’</sup>s 4Q23 GDP growth, driven mostly by an expected payback in agricultural output. Away from that sector, the economy expanded around a 2%ar during 2H23. We look for growth to reaccelerate above a 2%ar for the first half of this year, consistent with the PMI recovery and fundamentally healthy private-sector balance sheets. Meanwhile, Mexico<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth should snap back to a 3%ar this quarter on strong domestic demand and fiscal spending ahead of elections. Activity data also started on the right foot in Chile, with gains in manufacturing, mining, and retail sales. These should help drive growth to 2.6%ar in 1Q from just 0.4%ar last quarter.

## Economic Activity Tracking

This week we introduce a more comprehensive suite of activity trackers that showcase a subset of our quantitative tools. See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 1Q24 global real GDP growth ticked up 0.1%-pt this week to 2.6%ar (Fig. 1). The top-down global nowcaster held steady at 2.0%ar, suggesting 0.6%-pt of downside risk to global growth (Table 2).

Nearly two-thirds of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 1Q growth in only a third of these economies (Fig. 2).

Our CapexNow model estimate for 1Q24 global capex growth climbed to 3.4%ar this week following a rebound in the global capital goods PMI in February (Table 4).

This week<sup>’</sup>s key data release was February global manufacturing PMI, which bolstered confidence that the ongoing recovery in global industry can be sustained.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bond yields retraced some of the rise in the previous weeks, but remain elevated. With yields close to 2-month highs, markets pricing in a later start to easing and fewer cuts than we expect, our Treasury Client Survey is continuing to point to an elevated share of neutrals, and as we see softer-than-expected data in the coming week, we recommend adding duration in 5Y USTs. We also hold 5s/30s steepeners as a medium-term expression of our Fed view and as we see higher term premia over time given the rising share of price sensitive investors.

In the Euro area, a smaller-than-expected decline in CPI supports recent pushback from ECB speakers on near-term easing expectations, and we expect the ECB to remain firmly in data-watching mode at its March meeting. We keep strategic longs in 5Y Germany but add 3s/10s EUR swap curve bear flatteners as a tactical hedge. In the UK, we see little impact from the likely modest fiscal measures in the spring budget. We hold tactical Aug24/Nov24 MPC OIS flatteners and Mar24/Sep24 SONIA futures curve flatteners.

In EM, our EM client survey showed a slight reduction in already relatively modest net EM local duration longs, while longs in EM hard currency sovereigns rose to their highest level since Feb<sup>’</sup>23. We stay OW local bond duration, with OWs in Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Czechia, Poland, South Africa and Indonesia, partially offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Romania (EM Fixed Income Focus, Feb 15<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG bond spreads remained in a tight 8bp range in February and are trending modestly tighter MTD. In yet another reflection that supply follows demand, spreads are tighter despite record issuance for a February with \$194bn printing, which is 28% higher than the prior record of \$152bn set just last year. This is notable given that March is typically the busiest supply month of the year, averaging \$161bn over the past 4 years (ex-2020). Inflows have not only increased overall this year but are also stronger m/m, unlike last year when inflows tapered off somewhat from Jan to Feb. We continue to see valuations as being expensive, but yields remain attractive and risk assets more broadly remain on an uptrend. We also continue to view spreads as expensive here but recognize the risk that they could go tighter still if yields remain supportive and macro concerns continue to dissipate (Credit Market Outlook & Strategy, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

Euro HG spreads will likely trade range-bound over the near term between 130bp and 140bp, in our view. We revise up our full-year HG issuance forecast by €35bn to €550bn gross / €80bn net. This is driven by: 1) excess corporate cash balances being depleted; 2) M&A activity picking up; and 3) higher reverse Yankee issuance (European Credit Weekly, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/8bb6dc293483d3c0ef4248563f1001925c2aaf5cc7f05c29f4e85672e2f139b0.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

February registered a post-pandemic high in actual defaults and a 10-month high in volume affected by defaults/distressed transactions in HY and LL. However, bond and loan default rates both declined m/m due to a large default rolling off the 12-month rate calculation. Combined there were 12 defaults/distressed transactions totaling \$9.9bn in bonds and loans. Notably, the difference between par and issuer-based default rates is the highest on record and underscores the impact distressed exchanges have had on default activity over the last few years. The distressed universe of bonds and loans is at a low since May 2022 (Default Monitor, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

The main narrative at the SFIG conference was that spreads have significantly tightened in mortgage credit and other parts of SPG, but there does not look to be a big catalyst for widening unless Fed expectations are significantly reprice. Investors remain neutral on mortgage credit and saw some value in agency MBS, jumbo 2.0 and non-QM AAA/Mezz. Money managers continue to see inflows and the insurance bid for duration remains strong. Given these dynamics, new issue deals continue to be multiple times oversubscribed. Higher loan size borrowers appear to be driving

delinquencies higher in non-QM, which is surprising since a larger loan size is typically an indicator of borrower creditworthiness (RMBS Credit Commentary, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

## Currencies

Upcoming US data will set the tone for FX. We consider a broad roadmap for select outcomes. 2023 offers clues given some similarities, but differences will be relevant as well. The most disruptive outcome for FX would be a hawkish Fed repricing, which as in 2023 would result in broad USD strength. Unlike 2023, higher US yields should not automatically also result in positive returns from carry given thinner yield cushions.

Macro Trade Recommendations: Take profit on EUR/SEK cash shorts but stay long SEK vs CZK in cash and EUR in options. Stay modestly long USD vs EUR & CAD via options amid lingering US exceptionalism and prospects of tariffs. Long JPY vs EUR in cash, vs USD and CHF in options. Short EUR/CHF in cash. Stay OW EM FX (FX Markets Weekly, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

## Commodities

Moving along the electrification road. 21.6 million electrified vehicles (BEV, PHEV, hybrids) were likely sold worldwide in 2023 or 33% of total auto sales. The market is moving from early adopters to early mass majority in Europe and China, while the US remains a nascent market. Sales of battery EVs are still rising, but the growth rate appears to be easing, while hybrids emerge as a bridge technology to pure EVs. Fully or partially electric vehicles are now a meaningful share of the fleet, representing about 7% of the global fleet, likely shaving \~500 kbd off global gasoline demand between 2019 and 2023, with 400 kbd of those from the US and China. Increasing pressure from electrification and efficiency gains will likely lead to a first on record, outside of recession, contraction in global gasoline demand in 2025 and a cumulative loss of 0.9 mbd by 2030 (Oil Markets Weekly, Feb 26<sup>th</sup>).

How much is a deviation from average Chinese seasonal inventory builds worth? Metals demand has been somewhat slow to hit its stride post LNY holiday, though inventories are still showing relatively tame builds, despite higher production in China. Copper stock builds fell back in line with normal seasonality this week while primary aluminum and zinc builds continued to undershoot. This year, copper and zinc prices have outpaced implied performance from seasonal Chinese stock build deviations while aluminum has lagged (Metals Weekly, Mar 1<sup>st</sup>).

Qatar LNG capitalizes on US policy uncertainty. The energy minister of Qatar announced further expansion of the

country<sup>’</sup>s LNG export capacity by 16 mtpa “before the end of 2030.<sup>”</sup> The Qatari announcement comes just weeks after the Biden administration announced a pause in new LNG project approvals. As the new expansion is projected to come on line by 2030 and with no details known on financing, partnerships or contracting, we expect it to have no to minimal impact on our published global gas balances by 2030 (Global Natural Gas Flash Note, Feb 26<sup>th</sup>).

# DM easing: the whole is greater than the sum of the parts

While pushing back start date, markets still anticipate substantial 2024-25 DM easing

• Small but correlated economic surprises can generate large differences in CB outcomes

• A recession threat will leverage disinflation producing substantial easing

• Modest upside growth and inflation surprises to generate a hawkish lever ...

• … as it also alters views on r\*, u\* and bias of risk

In a note on DM central bank easing last December we emphasized that growth and inflation interactions are key in determining the start and speed of this year<sup>’</sup>s actions. If lower inflation combines with a material threat to the expansion we noted that “it would likely generate early and possibly substantial policy easing in the US and Western Europe.<sup>”</sup> By contrast, positive inflation dynamics amidst resilient growth was viewed as likely to deliver a “slow and shallow<sup>”</sup>response. It is thus no surprise that a recent combination of growth resilience and easing financial conditions has pushed back a rising tide of early easing optimism. With DM inflation firming last month markets have also reduced optimism about the size of the overall 2024-25 easing cycle (Figure 1).

Consensus expectations for a substantial easing cycle ahead have remained firmly in place, however, with market pricing incorporating a roughly 150bp decline in policy rates for the Fed, ECB, and BoE by the end of next year. Market pricing could incorporate the risk of aggressive easing in the event of a recession. But the motion of asset prices and bond yields suggests there is a close linkage between rising risk appetite and optimism that central banks can ease in an environment of sustained expansion.

Figure 1: Market pricing of forward policy curves %; pricing as of Jan 2 (dashed) and Feb 26 (solid)
![](images/4bfcafb266eb1197382d6b8b7c508672171b27ee1adcd492abeda1f9bf9c5ab2.jpg)

Current expectations likely overstate the magnitude of the coming 2024-25 DM easing cycle, absent a genuine threat to the expansion. An “immaculate<sup>”</sup>disinflation that returns inflation to target should deliver material easing even if it is delivered at a slow pace. But an even modest degree of DM inflation stickiness—say at around a 2.5% pace—will likely limit the magnitude of easing in an environment of solid growth and labor markets tightness. In this regard, relatively small but sustained and correlated surprises in economic outcomes will likely produce large differences in cumulative action. Just as a recession threat will likely lever a disinflationary easing, modest upside surprises to growth, wage and price inflation will likely be magnified as it also influences central bankers<sup>’</sup> assessments of risk and the unobservable estimates of neutral rates and slack.

## Easing without recession usually shallow

History stands on the side of an early reversal of a large policy tightening. Following episodes over the last 50 years in which the US FOMC has tightened more than 200bp, an easing has generally been underway within a year from the rate peak (Figure 2). Rate hiking in Western Europe during this period has tended to be more extended and exhibit less consistency, but still does not show episodes in policy rates remaining at cyclical peaks for a significant period.

Figure 2: US policy rates around tightening cycles
![](images/143282667f1979ce45e35f946222e9d254592ef0e629cbb1ed2b33be8eded890.jpg)

While an unwind of tightening cycles generally comes quickly, the magnitude of the easing varies. During the US softlanding episodes in which expansions were sustained well after tightening, the Fed eased roughly 200bp in 1984-85 and 75bp in 1995-96 in the 18 months from the rate peak. In the four episodes over this period in which tightening was followed by a recession, easing was generally quite modest until labor markets weakened.

Two related arguments have reinforced expectations for a substantial policy easing over 2024-25 absent a slide into recession. The first is that the inflation surge of 2021-23 that prompted an unprecedented, synchronized DM policy tightening is fading fast as negative supply shocks related to the pandemic and Russia<sup>’</sup>s invasion of Ukraine have been removed from from the scene. The sharp inflation slide that took hold in 2H23 has raised expectations that we are in an unprecedented environment in which high inflation can be tamed without generating a significant period of weak growth (Figure 3).

Figure 3: DM core CPI and u-rate forecast through end-2024
![](images/a5d3e07021d57604130553a2a888c0be89a15c58ff7c5707bd4e064b695bb8e4.jpg)

The second is that neutral real policy rates were pushed significantly lower over the past decade and, as a result, the current stance of monetary policy is materially restrictive. This is the view of our economists who currently estimate policy rates to be significantly higher than neutral (Figure 4). This view is consistent with policy calibrating easy in the face of a significant but incomplete disinflation.

Figure 4: Policy rate deviation from neutral %-pt, based on JPM economist neutral forecasts
![](images/78ae8e1c01cfb5dcec5333960002642c968f8fb82fb62cb350c3d609b927308d.jpg)

## Not so fast: Inflation likely sticky

With central bank rhetoric reinforcing disinflation optimism and fueling easing expectations, markets turned into the new year building in expectations of both early and substantial easing by US and Western European central banks. Indeed, in early January more than 200bp of easing was incorporated into markets for the Fed, ECB, and BoE.

From a top-down perspective we have pushed back against a large easing cycle, absent a slide into recession. On the inflation front, the slide in core inflation during 2H23 was a highly encouraging development (Figure 5). Following two years in which DM core inflation hovered close to 5%, it fell to a

2.9%ar in 2H23. As we turned into the new year, many forecasters seized on the inertial properties of inflation to extrapolate this impressive slide further. However, we viewed progress as likely to stall in 1H23 and incoming inflation supports our views. Two judgments have informed these views.

Figure 5: Global core CPI - ex China and Türkiye %3m, saar
![](images/c652c22632e2d054637873f3981035dbfbbb3e0e7fd0772879de7a96cda9f299.jpg)

Core goods disinflation is ending. The downshift in global core inflation in 2H23 was narrowly concentrated in goods prices where the removal of bottlenecks, falling commodity prices, and weak goods demand generated a synchronized global slide in core goods CPI growth. For some months, this global disinflationary impulse has been fading as goods demand has picked up and input costs have firmed. Excluding a large drop in US used car prices, January inflation reports point to a broad stabilization in global goods prices. Importantly, this message is consistent with the signals from business surveys and trade prices. The US has seen a sharp rise in imported consumer goods prices as we turn into the new year, even as China continues to exert a deflationary impulse (Figure 6).

Figure 6: US core goods price pressures
![](images/321746474b050111897d93769a9bc532d81bab74b0a52b595dc0b9bf8df8d868.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

Limited fundamental support for service price disinflation. Forecasting the trajectory for service price inflation is more difficult and subject to idiosyncratic developments. Nevertheless, strong growth in service sector activity, tight labor markets, and elevated labor costs gains argue against a follow-through of last year<sup>’</sup>s goods price slide to service sector disinflation in 1H24. Globally core service price inflation aligned with our forecast as we moved through last quarter, stabilizing at an elevated 4.2%ar. However, Euro area core HICP and US “supercore<sup>”</sup> (services ex rent) core PCE delivered significant downside surprises. We have faded these moves and January releases point to a significant rebound in both cases. In the US, the swing is dramatic.

## Different challenges across the Atlantic

Despite an unprecedented, synchronized tightening in global monetary policy over 2022-23 global growth has proceeded at an above-trend pace in recent quarters, with our economists forecast for GDP gains moving steadily higher in recent months. By itself, this outcome has raises questions regarding assessments of where neutral policy rates stand. This message is reinforced by the fact that tightening monetary conditions—elevated borrowing costs and more restrictive bank lending standards—have not translated into a material tightening in financial conditions or emergence of financial stress (Figure 7).

Figure 7: G4 bank lending standards and global financial stress
![](images/d8e20b63e60525a5a184f4b7923a8a11f7fd7838e87eab7d72901a4d769833c8.jpg)

While DM growth surprises bias risk in the direction of higher neutral policy rate estimates, the linkage between growth performance and the DM easing cycle points to a very different set of pressures on central bankers in the US and Western Europe.

Although growth forecasts have been revised higher over the past year, the magnitude of upward revision (by JPM economists and central banks) has been far greater in the US than elsewhere (Figure 8). As such, it is in the US where growth performance is most obviously challenging the Fed<sup>’</sup>s SEP forecast of view a 2.5% nominal long-term rate.

Figure 8: J.P. Morgan GDP forecast evolution %oya ending 2Q24; forecast by date made
![](images/fd22846a140654524ddce91fdef6b2224072642488e7398fc53f27eb910eff2f.jpg)

Supply-side performance is tempering the relative pressure generated by the US-Western Europe growth gap. Robust US demand growth has been delivered in an environment of strong labor force gains and elevated productivity increases. As a result, the US employment rate has been broadly stable over the past year (Figure 9). And while wages continue to rise at an elevated pace, unit labor cost gains cooled to 2.3% over the past four quarters (Figure 10).

Figure 9: DM unemployment rate relative to pre-pandemic %-pt deviation from 4Q19
![](images/466229de5620da38e85eed0d8f22b0878440d9bc04f4c50da9db2c646e5fa5ec.jpg)
Figure 10: DM unit labor costs

% change over 4Q; latest: US (4Q23), Euro area (3Q), UK (2Q)
![](images/b0c6c6da1a07be322b87bbf6a7b6612b862d60b90f4b4e019df4ed699d8179f8.jpg)

Meanwhile, the Euro area and UK have been stagnant over the past four quarters, putting pressure on any reassessment of neutral policy rates higher. However, weak supply-side performance has not translated a growth stall into a labor market easing. And in both economies wage gains as well as unit labor cost increases remain well above levels that central bank view as consistent with their inflation targets.

## The Taylor school for rules

Translating these considerations into a path for policy rates is not straightforward. A balanced Taylor rule, in which policy rates are determined giving equal weight to inflation and unemployment gaps, provides a simple framework for evaluating the path ahead (Figure 11). Applying our economists forecasts for key parameters—which incorporate a significant inflation decline in all cases—the Taylor rule estimate places appropriate policy rates at 3% in the US, 3.5% in the Euro area and close to 5% in the UK in mid-2025. The somewhat higher European Taylor rule estimates—which stand above our economists<sup>’</sup> policy rate forecasts—reflect a larger unemployment gap relative to the US as both Euro area and UK unemployment rates are estimated to remain well below estimates of NAIRU.

Figure 11: Taylor rule estimates %; dots for actual and forecasted policy rate
![](images/4bddfd42adc661adc6565cb5dd4b974fdeb9b84f01d861ff21158a1ccff1207d.jpg)

A Taylor rule is a starting point, rather than end, for discussion about appropriate policy rates. The discussion above highlights a significant debate about whether measures of r\* are understated, particularly in the US. In addition, there is a significant debate about the appropriate measure of the output gap. Based on our economists<sup>’</sup> unemployment gap measures, the US economy is running roughly at full employment while the Euro area and UK are running hotter. However, GDP output gaps tell a different story (Figure 12). Most estimates place the US output gap in positive territory at the end of 2019. Since then, the economy has risen roughly 1.5%-pts above its pre-pandemic potential path. By contrast, GDP growth has fallen far short of its potential path in both the Euro area and UK since the pandemic. While relatively strong (weak) US (Western Europe) supply-side performance explains part of this gap, there is a strong presumption that unemployment gaps might understate Western European slack while overstating it for the US.

Figure 12: DM GDP relative to pre-pandemic trend %-pt deviation from 4Q19
![](images/ba2d6b599b20595e47fff9a96fbb235ad82370c82676ba4b94c95e88d2c23fc0.jpg)

Both neutral policy rates and output gaps are unobservable and central bank judgments will be determined based on the evolution of observable outcomes in growth, inflation and labor markets. It is important to recognize that relatively small but correlated surprises in economic outcomes can produce large differences in central bank judgments on appropriate policy. To see this, we consider the sensitivity of Taylor rule rate estimates for the US to changes in the FOMC<sup>’</sup>s Survey of Economic Projections (SEP) for 2025 (Table 1). Applying current projections together with an estimated 1% real policy rate—modestly above the SEP long-run rate r\* estimate—yields a 3.3% implied policy rate. This Taylor rule prescription aligns with current FOMC guidance —the SEP projects 3.6% in 4Q25—as well as J.P. Morgan forecasts.

However, the picture changes significantly if perceptions shift modestly across multiple fronts. Outcomes in which the unemployment rate is perceived to remain unchanged at its current level (3.7%) and core PCE inflation is expected to settle modestly higher than forecast (2.5%) would likely also prompt a modest upward revision to estimates of the nearterm neutral policy rate. While each of these has a small impact on prescribed policy rates for next year, the set of correlated shifts presented in Table 1 push the prescribed policy rate back toward 5%. While still consistent with rate cuts year, this exercise highlights that the recent strength of US growth and employment has increased the risk of a reassessment that can challenge optimism about the magnitude of the easing cycle ahead.

Table 1: US Taylor rule policy rate
<table><tr><td colspan="2">Appropriate policy rate, balanced rule</td></tr><tr><td>Baseline using FOMC 2025 SEP</td><td>3.3%</td></tr><tr><td>Adjusted for:</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Higher r* (1.25%)</td><td>3.6%</td></tr><tr><td>.+ unchanged u-rate (3.7%)</td><td>4.4%</td></tr><tr><td>...+ Sticky PCE inflation (2.5%)</td><td>4.8%</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics. Baseline uses FOMC SEP 2025 forecasts: 4.1% u-rate; 2.2% core PCE. Assumes r\*=1%.
Taylor rule: ffr = r\* + infl + 0.5\*(inf - 2%) - 2\*(ur - 4.1%)

## US: The capital-labor division of high inflation

Labor costs account for a little less than half of the inflation this cycle; capital captured most of the gains

• Going forward, rising profit margins are unlikely to be a major source of inflation pressure

This should generally leave the spotlight on unit labor costs for the next act in the inflation story

The inflation episode of the last few years is commonly looked at through the perspective of which prices are going up. A less commonly examined perspective is to look at who is receiving the income from these higher prices. There are a few ways to dissect inflation this way, but they generally indicate that capital received most of the higher nominal income from the burst of inflation, particularly in the early stages of that burst.

Despite this finding, we think it makes sense to remain focused on labor costs to see where inflation is going. While capital has been the beneficiary thus far, history suggests the capital share of prices is leveling off and any inflation followthrough will accrue to labor.

## Cui bono

Ever since Powell<sup>’</sup>s November 2022 Brookings speech, commentators have been discussing core inflation in three broad buckets: core goods, housing services, and core services exhousing (or super-core). However, for business output prices one can also divide price increase into unit labor costs and unit non-labor payments. When the business in question is a corporation the price increase can be further divided into unit labor costs, unit profits, and unit non-labor costs (the latter being depreciation, interest, rent, and indirect taxes).

Figure 1: Business output prices
![](images/5dbbf58a0950d100554fcf1680839d66783131eabc315558b35a3e4690acbe0e.jpg)

Before going further, we should emphasize that we are investigating business output prices. While owners<sup>’</sup> imputed rents are an important category of consumer prices, they are generally not the output price of a business or corporation. For this reason business output prices look more similar to the US HICP (Harmonized Index of Consumer Prices) which, like its trans-Atlantic counterparts, differs primarily from the CPI through its exclusion of recently-hot owners<sup>’</sup> imputed rents. So unlike CPI or PCE inflation the disinflation in business prices is mission accomplished: the four-quarter change in nonfarm business output prices is 2.3% and for nonfinancial corporate output prices it is 1.5% (Figure 1).

We first look at output prices for the nonfarm business sector (Figure 2). For this sector price increases are attributable to two sources: unit labor costs (which is labor compensation adjusted for productivity) and unit non-labor payments. Unit non-labor payments are basically payments to capital. They are not payments for intermediate inputs: one business<sup>’</sup>s intermediate input is another business<sup>’</sup>s output. After consolidating across the sector those payments cancel out. Similarly, because these are the prices of business value added, the effect of imported price inflation is stripped out.

<sup>label</sup>Figure 2: Nonfarm business price drivers
![](images/41e6f04c24aefaf0f30aa483f76caa05a9f563d16319542a70f4a55b690eeb5b.jpg)

Since the beginning of 2021 the output prices of nonfarm businesses increased at a 5.2% annual rate: 38% of this is due to unit labor costs, 62% is due to unit non-labor payments.

We turn next to the non-financial corporate sector (Figure 3). For this sector the economic data break out the profits component. They also treat costs from equity<sup>’</sup>s perspective, and as mentioned unit non-labor costs are depreciation, interest, rent, and indirect taxes.

Figure 3: Nonfinancial corporate price drivers Over year-ago %-pt contribution
![](images/9daf9ec33dc0997da9c045d0165e1d3e9be4257a39f3de80495d2d728be45420.jpg)

Since the beginning of 2021 output prices for the nonfinancial corporate sector increased at a 5.1% annual rate; unit labor costs accounted for 45% of that increase, unit non-labor costs for 25%, and profits for 30%. The rather large number for unit non-labor costs is largely the result of the accounting for the Paycheck Protection Program (PPP). If we start from the beginning of 2020, the inflation share of unit labor costs goes to 53%, unit non-labor costs to 12%, and profits 35%.

At first blush, these figures might appear to support the arguments of those who have accused business of “greedflation.<sup>”</sup> But the point here isn<sup>’</sup>t to assign blame, but to understand with the goal of projecting the path ahead. And, in some ways the expansion of profit margins in this cycle can be seen as a normal cyclical development. Historically, early on in business cycles profit margins expand, only to eventually get squeezed as the cycle matures (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Corporate profit share of sector prices %, bars indicate recessions
![](images/ed4dc9a655ea5363f23c1bb9a4910c181ef1d3357e7705542779991b620d9271.jpg)

In this cycle the profit share jumped more abruptly than has been the historical pattern, but qualitatively the historical pattern of an early increase in the profit share played out again this cycle. This historical pattern may also offer clues to where the profit share of prices is heading. After the initial early jump, the profit share has plateaued, and the record suggests that as the cycle matures the share should go down, or at least not increase further.

Turning to non-labor costs, the lagged effect of higher interest rates could be a source of upward pressure on prices. But the largest component of non-labor costs is depreciation, and there is no reason to expect anything particularly notable here. That leaves labor costs. In the year ending 4Q23 unit labor costs increased a relatively benign 2.3%. This reflected the difference between compensation per hour increasing 5.1% and productivity, which grew 2.7% (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Drivers of unit labor costs, nonfarm business %ch, over year-ago
![](images/795ecd67e87c4cedcb0a4a743ab127b6a81c4fb434b240e59c8d83ed51f56629.jpg)

Looking forward, it is reasonable to expect slowing in the growth of compensation per hour, if for no other reason than that it is growing faster than other wage measures such as the ECI (4.2%), the Atlanta Fed wage tracker (4.7%), and average hourly earnings (4.5%). It would be nice to think productivity will sustain its recent strong growth, but we<sup>’</sup>re not sold yet and expect moderation this year. So with a different mix, we expect unit labor costs to continue growing in the neighborhood of 2% this year. This should allow business output price inflation to remain tame.

A friend of labor might ask why it<sup>’</sup>s on workers to ensure disinflation is maintained: after all capital was the major beneficiary of the <sup>’</sup>21-<sup>’</sup>22 inflation surge. Even if one is sympathetic to this argument, economic reasoning suggests this would be better dealt with by fiscal or judicial authorities—the blunt instrument of monetary policy is poorly suited to guide the division of national income. Moreover, and referring back to Figure 4, the historical record suggests that whenever the next recession comes, capital will bear a larger share of the burden.

## Tunisia: Of oil and olive oil

External balances improved in 2023 despite lack of IMF deal

Trade deficit shrank by over 5% of GDP thanks to positive terms-of-trade and external demand

• Commodity price and external demand risks remain, but external risks appear manageable

• A narrower current account deficit will be key as 2024 external funding sources are not fully defined yet

Tunisia recorded a large current account improvement in 2023 which helped the country to accumulate over US\$1bn in FX reserves despite lack of IMF funding. While uncertainties over external financing linger — we don<sup>’</sup>t expect an IMF deal in 2024 either — the accumulated buffers should help mitigate risks. CAD is expected to remain manageable at below US\$2bn, although volatility could arise from external risks.

## Narrower CAD allays repayment fears

After recording a wide current account deficit (CAD) worth 8.6% of GDP in 2022, in early 2023 we forecasted an improvement to slightly above 5% of GDP (link). Despite being relatively bullish at the time (consensus saw CAD over 8%), the improvement surpassed expectations. The deficit came in at just 2.6% of GDP last year, enabling Tunisia to report a strong external picture despite IMF support not materializing. Official reserves increased by US\$1.2bn. These were propped up by funding from multilateral and bilateral partners, with the government able to secure net external borrowing of US\$1.3bn, as well as other financial flows. Tunisia repaid its EUR500mn October-23 and EUR850mn February-24 Eurobonds which traded 40 points below-par in June 2022, and serviced debt for a total of US\$3.8bn in 2023.

## Strong tourism and large trade narrowing

Structurally, Tunisia benefits from remittances inflows and a sizable services surplus, while a primary income deficit and a wide goods<sup>’</sup> trade imbalance keep the CA in negative territory (Figure 1). Awaiting final 4Q23 figures, as the CA likely reported a narrow deficit for the quarter, we estimate remittances contracted to below US\$3bn in 2023, still well above the pre-pandemic norm of \~US\$2bn. Meanwhile, the services surplus increased as tourism receipts reached US\$2.3bn in 2023 - an increase of almost US\$1bn compared to 2022. This was possible thanks to a strong return of tourists from neighbouring Libya and Algeria. The primary income deficit widened slightly as external interest repayments moved higher. Despite the increase, a large amount of concessional/nearconcessional external debt still allowed Tunisia to keep interest costs low, close to 3%.

By far the largest shift between 2022 and 2023 came in the trade balance, with the goods trade deficit shrinking by over US\$2.6bn (5.4% of GDP). This was achieved thanks to a large pick-up in exports (+8%) while imports were down by 5%. A positive swing in terms-of-trade (ToT), supportive external and weak domestic demand played a key role, and some of these factors are likely to remain in place in 2024.

![](images/97ba8226e2286f707338912da6f04768d56038caf99833dccddffcda86370aae.jpg)

## Oil down, (olive) oil up

To better understand how the trade balance improved so considerably and what this means for the outlook, we took a close look at components. Figure 2 shows that the deterioration in 2022 was largely due to elevated hydrocarbon and food imports driven by high international prices. In the same vein, the US\$2.6bn 2023 trade balance improvement was largely due to a reversal of these dynamics alongside better ToT. On top of this, substantial help came from exports of electrical items and olive oil. For the former, we estimate a revenue pick-up close to 20%, 15%-pts of which was explained by higher volumes and the remainder by higher prices. On the other hand, a remarkable rise in the price of olive oil meant that exports increased by over US\$0.4bn (+50%), despite production being down on the year.

![](images/3109cb168d6a6705dc83ea785d26d764d997e0fd5f9c4d225207ab67aec7d0d5.jpg)

Overall, favourable price dynamics were the crux of the improvement (Figure 3). Nevertheless, improvements also took place in trade less affected by international prices. As Figure 1 shows, the trade deficit related to more demand driven items or <sup>‘</sup>other goods<sup>’</sup> contracted sharply too, continuing a trend that began before the pandemic and symptomatic of limited economic growth spurring some import compression.

Figure 3: Trade balance and terms of trade
![](images/b17d2e8431681ba1a95ab6f0316bb898a499ea027e2cbe6043d6e1a8ae3854fa.jpg)
Source: BCT, INS, J.P. Morgan - sesaonally adjusted figures

## ST outlook favourable despite volatility risk

Tunisia remains exposed to potential commodity price shocks and weak external demand. Table 1 shows sensitivities for three key commodities which played an important role in the recent turnaround: oil, olive oil, and wheat. A 10% swing in prices compared to our current baseline would deliver sizeable effects on the trade balance, ranging from +0.3% to -0.4% of GDP. A return to pre-pandemic prices would also imply some significant shocks (see right-hand side columns). However, with the exception of wheat where prices are gradually moving lower after the 2022 Ukraine war shock, prices are not close to such levels and current dynamics do not suggest a near-term return. Regarding wheat, after a very weak local harvest in 2023 due to unfavorable weather and drought, local production should rebound this year, allowing for lower import volumes.

Table 1: Trade balance sensitivity to price change of key commodities
<table><tr><td colspan="3">10% price increase from current 2024 baseline</td><td colspan="2">2015-2019 average prices</td></tr><tr><td>Trade impact*</td><td>US$bn</td><td>as % of GDP</td><td>US$bn</td><td>as % of GDP</td></tr><tr><td>Net oil trade</td><td>-0.20</td><td>-0.36</td><td>0.60</td><td>1.10</td></tr><tr><td>Olive oil export</td><td>0.16</td><td>0.30</td><td>-0.93</td><td>-1.71</td></tr><tr><td>Wheat import</td><td>-0.11</td><td>-0.20</td><td>0.23</td><td>0.42</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan \*Exercise assumes no changes to volumes.
2024 avg price baseline: Brent \$83/bl; Olive oil EUR 632/100kg; FAO cereal -8% from 2023

The olive oil harvest is completed between 4Q and early 1Q in the Mediterranean basin, so estimates for 2023/24 production are already available. Despite a partial recovery in the top two producers (Spain and Italy), levels look likely to remain below those of the 2021/22 season. Moreover, bad harvests in the third (Turkey) and fifth (Greece) largest producers will likely leave overall global production below the already-poor 2022/23 season when global production contracted by 25%. Tunisia is expected to recover some of its lost production, so its global share should increase to around 8%. These dynamics should translate into another strong year in

terms of olive oil revenues. International prices are currently at all-time highs and unlikely to move significantly lower until summer when indications of next seasons harvest will begin to form (link).

On the non-commodity side, demand is largely driven by the Euro Area outlook which drives the large textile and electrical sectors. At the moment, we expect Euro Area to record anaemic annual growth of just 0.4% in 2024, implying no real boost to these exports. On the import side, we see Tunisia<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth improving this year, but largely as a function of a mechanical recovery in agricultural output. As a result, we don<sup>’</sup>t expect any large expansion in demand-related imports.

## Manageable CA while looking for financing

Given the above, we view the CAD as manageable in 2024. We pencil in some moderate widening to US\$1.9bn (3.5% of GDP) from US\$1.3bn in 2023, remaining far below previous years<sup>’</sup> deficits. Despite the benign baseline, we remain concerned about the potential impact of shocks mentioned.

On the financial account, the full 2024 budget reported government external amortizations of US\$3.1bn, about US\$1bn higher than 2023. A relatively narrow CAD would therefore continue to be important in securing Tunisia<sup>’</sup>s external position, especially as questions remain over availability of fund ing. The budget reports US\$3.2bn in “undefined<sup>”</sup> external financing, as authorities continue to negotiate with partners over sourcing additional hard currency. As the largest single amortization came due early in the year, authorities used the new BCT financing facility to access foreign reserves and repay the EUR850mn Eurobond. We expect some of the US\$3.2bn gap to be plugged during the year as Tunisia has a positive track record in terms of securing financing, but expect that total official reserves will have to contract by about US\$700mn in 2024. This would leave Tunisia with a still comfortable reserves level of around US\$8.4bn.

Figure 4: Tunisia gross international reserves
![](images/667b3f74498a5c7ea7726de1f4d4736c7f2d3715b8ab41c56a7e2918a2e2a34e.jpg)
Source: BCT, J.P. Morgan - Last data point for February (JPM est)

## Australia: Leads, lags, and labor cost growth

• Annual unit labor cost growth is running at three times its 2010-19 average pace...

• ...raising questions about whether wage growth is consistent with the inflation target

• But labor costs are not helpful for forecasting inflation

Instead, labor cost growth tends to lag inflation, probably reflecting delays in the wage setting process

In 4Q23, Australian annual wage growth exceeded CPI growth for the first time since 1Q21. We expect inflation to fall more quickly than wage growth from here, dampening risks of a price-wage spiral. But as long as wage growth runs comfortably above 3% and the level of trend productivity growth remains unclear, some uncertainty will persist about the consistency of labor cost growth with the inflation target.

What is the usual relationship between inflation and growth in labor costs? In this note, we find changes in unit labor cost (ULC) growth are not particularly helpful for forecasting changes in inflation. But ULC growth does increase when GDP growth is too low, relative to its long-term relationship with inflation. These results seem perverse to the usual wageprice calculus, in which labor market tightness generates higher wage growth and, in turn, inflation. One explanation is that if firms set prices based on their expectations of future wage growth, sluggishness in the wage setting process mean observed wages appear to follow prices with a lag.

Figure 1: Australian headline CPI and unit labor costs growth %oya
![](images/15a7b451971a08c41216c2913910619814fa8a689b0a174a53176de8a5b96d14.jpg)

## Constant ups and downs

Changes in firms<sup>’</sup> labor costs depend on both the change in employee compensation and productivity. If wages rise one for one with productivity then a firm<sup>’</sup>s cost per unit need not change. The ABS<sup>’</sup>s nominal unit labor cost series scales average labor costs (employee compensation divided by total hours worked by employees) by average labor productivity (real GDP divided by hours worked), so provides a productivity-adjusted labor cost measure. Figure 1 shows that, aside from the wrinkles associated with lockdowns, ULC growth has risen and fallen in sympathy with headline inflation since 2021. A tentative conclusion is that rising labor costs were passed onto final prices, generating positive co-movement between ULC growth and inflation.

Taking the difference between inflation and labor cost growth provides a measure of the growth of firms<sup>’</sup> markups. If markups tend to be constant over time, then a rise in labor cost growth may spur a rise in inflation, so that markup (growth) remains at its long-run average. Figure 2 plots the difference between quarterly headline inflation and ULC growth.

Figure 2: Quarterly growth in firms' markups
![](images/37bbf5cfbee4daa36248303e176d23d20abdf301abbb2b6f9698e157bd4deaec.jpg)

The markup series fluctuates around a constant mean close to zero. This indicates that markups have tended to grow at a very low, stable rate over time, but it doesn<sup>’</sup>t shed any light on the adjustment mechanism that maintains this long-run rate. Either inflation or ULC growth (or both) may adjust to keep markup growth stable. If inflation adjusts, then changes in ULC growth should be helpful for forecasting inflation.

## Tests of significance

We can get a better idea of the relationship between inflation and ULC growth by modeling changes in both series as a function of their own lags, lags of each other and markup growth. If the effects of changes in ULC growth on fluctuations in inflation are significantly different from zero, then ULC growth should contain predictive content about future inflation. Or, if inflation tends to rise (fall) when markup growth is below (above) its long-run rate, ULC growth should also be helpful for forecasting inflation. We estimate the model using data on quarterly CPI and ULC growth from 3Q86- 4Q19. We include four lags of the change in inflation and ULC growth, and the markup series enters with a one period lag.<sup>1</sup>

Figure 3: Significance of unit labor cost growth on inflation changes p-value
![](images/2437ae653e63ce1a8d1bf44f868ef5d4c9f27033a739fa2d8475512ecace2868.jpg)

Figure 3 shows the p-value from the test that the coefficients on lagged changes in ULC growth are (jointly) significantly different from zero <sup>2</sup>. Since the p-value is greater than 0.1, the effect of lagged changes in ULC growth on changes in inflation does not appear statistically different from zero (at the 10% level). Still, ULC growth should still be helpful for forecasting inflation if inflation reacts significantly to deviations in mark-up growth from their long run-rate. Again, however, we don<sup>’</sup>t find that deviations in markup growth have an effect on inflation that are significantly different from zero (Figure 3 again). Taking the results together, we don<sup>’</sup>t find much evidence that ULC growth is useful for predicting inflation.

Figure 4: Significance of inflation on unit labor cost growth changes p-value
![](images/1b76abd32f5ee995000aa71c39c6e47458d46f0fa3e5294d956345a6b2951b22.jpg)

Figure 4 shows that the effects of lagged fluctuations in inflation, on changes in ULC growth are not significantly different from zero at the 10% level. But the p-value on the markup growth term in the ULC growth equation is very small, so we can say with some confidence that deviations in markup growth from its long-run equilibrium have a non-zero effect on ULC growth. Since the effect of lagged deviations in markup growth on ULC growth is positive, a rise in inflation that pushes markup growth above its long-run average is associated with a rise in ULC growth in the following period. So the model describes a world in which inflation appears to pull ULC growth higher/lower, more so than wage costs pushing inflation higher.

## Forward thinking

The results above suggest that ULC growth is a lagging indicator, and inflation is helpful for forecasting ULC growth via their long-run relationship. This is a fairly common finding in the literature (e.g., Hu and Toussaint-Comeau [2010] find similar results in the US) and is consistent with models in which prices are updated more frequently than wages. In this environment, forward-looking firms adjust their prices in response to changes in expectations about future wage growth. If firms expect higher wage growth in the future, inflation will rise and wages, which are updated more slowly, will follow with a lag.

Figure 5: Frequency of price changes per annum
![](images/ae7e84754939900b26826292a8399d300f933e5aec9122fe210a8a3884468a4c.jpg)
Source: RBA Bulletin June 2010.

How frequently are prices and wages updated in Australia? Estimates are a little dated but prices do appear to be reset more frequently than wages, which supports the argument above. RBA liaison estimates from 2010 suggested just above 40% of firms reset their prices more than once a year, and 25% of firms changed their prices more than once a month (Figure 5). On wages, we have noted a number of times throughout this cycle that wages in Australia are comparatively slower to reset vs. other DMs due to the large proportion (\~34% as of 4Q23) of longer-dated enterprise bargaining agreements. Consistent with this, the ABS estimated in 2016 that, on average, wages were updated just under once every five quarters.

The failure of ULC growth to predict inflation suggests that we shouldn<sup>’</sup>t put much weight on recent labor cost data to assess whether productivity-adjusted wage growth will settle at a target consistent level. But, given wages appear to follow prices with a lag, continued moderation in inflation should give some comfort that labor cost growth will continue to move toward target consistent levels. Our expectation is that nominal wage growth falls to around 3.5%oya by end 2024. We think this level is consistent with the inflation target, as productivity growth tends to be counter-cyclical in Australia, and the labor market is loosening.

## Korea: Cyclical recovery of external balance

• Merchandise trade surplus is expected to rise with tech-sector recovery and oil deficit stabilization

• Service trade deficit should widen with post-pandemic normalcy, yet income surplus should provide an offset

• Modestly higher net financial outflows are expected, yet FX reserve should recover on net

Korea<sup>’</sup>s external balance has been subject to two drags over the last couple of years, namely terms of trade headwinds and sluggish exports. These resulted in an unusual fall of FX reserves in 2022/23. However, the worst of the drag from the terms of trade and tech cycle appears to have passed. This implies larger current account surpluses in 2024/25 on top of better-than-expected annual 2023 CA surplus (Figure 1). Implications on the FX market will depend on other factors such as financial outflows and hedging behaviour, etc., as well, and the cyclical recovery of external balance will provide more flexibility to align macroeconomic policies to domestic conditions.

Figure 1: Current account balance
![](images/92249498f52c58e98de1e13cda868afa27a44f3d043239928087e255549edc6a.jpg)

## Goods surplus to rise on tech

Key for Korea<sup>’</sup>s merchandise goods balance is whether the non-oil surplus can sufficiently offset the oil trade deficit. The sharp fall of goods trade surplus in 2022 was due to: i) the rise in oil trade deficit thanks to a spike in global crude oil prices and ii) the fall in tech exports value from 2H22 to 1Q23 (Figure 2). Nominal tech exports bottomed out in 2Q23. The oil trade deficit should stabilize around the current level if we are correct in assuming that global crude oil price should stay near 80\$/bbl for 2024; which implies a continued recovery in the goods trade surplus.

In real volume growth terms, we expect that tech exports growth should turn up to about 11% annual growth in 2024 after a brief contraction in 2023, returning to about an average annual growth pre-COVID pace in 2016-18. That growth would buffer a projected slowdown in non-tech real exports due to slower overall GDP growth in major trading partners. Real import growth is expected to slow in 2024 due to subdued domestic demand growth. Hence real net exports should be the major contributor to overall GDP growth (JPMe 2.2%y/y).

Figure 2: Oil trade balace vs. tech exports US\$ bn, sa, both axes
![](images/551e7c6cb727eeb92b07beb05234955ef2fe5231b6ce40c1e761be30d49f5a53.jpg)

The price effect should also gradually turn favourable to the goods balance surplus in 2024, as the terms of trade is expected to crawl up slowly after 2022<sup>’</sup>s sharp deterioration. The current level (and a projected modest recovery going forward) of the terms of trade index implies about US\$13-14bn merchandise trade surplus in each quarter of 2024, suggesting about US\$56bn annual merchandise trade surplus (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Merchandise balance and terms of trade
![](images/61b4874171b7f57b8501fa1e0d6ef370d54bf2a5b46b66a8d95a57ebac96ce10.jpg)

## Non-goods balance to keep a small surplus

The non-goods trade balance, which includes service and income accounts, shifted to an unusually large surplus in 2022 (US\$10.2bn) from a US\$38.4bn deficit in 2017, largely driven by transportation and travel service accounts. The large swing was due to the pandemic disruption of international travel in 2020/21 (given Korea is a net travel service importer), and due to the rise in transportation service fee during the post-pandemic logistics disruption. As these COVID-related disruptions fade (with recovery of international travel and fall in shipping fees), we expect that the service account deficit should widen modestly to around US\$29bn from US\$26bn deficit in 2023 and US\$7bn in 2022.

Offsetting the gradually rising service account deficits, the income account surplus is expected to rise further in 2024. The primary income surplus rose sharply after the pandemic, mainly due to a strong rise in foreign direct investment (FDI) income receipts (US\$36.3bn in 2023 vs US\$16.7bn in 2019). FDI income (and portfolio/other investment earnings) should remain elevated in 2024/25, given the strong rise of outbound investment flows in recent years (implying an elevated stock of assets). On net, the non-goods trade balance is expected to keep a small surplus of about US\$1bn in 2024, which is similar to 2023 but lower than 2022<sup>’</sup>s US\$10bn surplus.

## Outbound financial flows to modestly rise

The current account (CA) recovery in 2024 should be partly offset by financial account outflows. Most of the CA surplus has been recycled into private foreign investment since 2010, and the former fell short of the latter in 2022-23, leading to a reduction in reserve assets. In 2024, we expect net financial outflows to grow by US\$ 8.8bn to US\$ 44.8bn, as cross-border investment recovers from both sides with the peak of global monetary tightening likely reached. That said, this is smaller than the expected US\$ 21.6bn gain of the CA surplus, implying a modest recovery of reserve assets in 2024 after unusual drawdowns in the previous two years.

The largest portion of financial outflows is attributed to locals purchasing foreign securities. Outflows for bonds have been muted since 2020 after being led by insurers in 2015-19, due to valuation losses from interest rate hikes and increased hedging costs, so locals have recently focused on overseas stocks. The National Pension Service (NPS) continued to increase its foreign stock investment in 2022-23, when others scaled back, supported by consistent pension contributions and its medium-term strategy to increase overseas investment (to 60% of AUM by 2028 from the current 52%). With a stable source of funds, NPS has been broadly on track with its plan, which allocated KRW 35.7tn in foreign stocks for 2024, up from KRW 33.4tn in 2023 (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Outflows for foreign stock investment by NPS
![](images/7cf70d8ab303a0cebce64c18db9358511faf81e012535b5f0cd5678bc264cb11.jpg)

In the private sector (previously contributing two-thirds of stock outflows at the peak of 2020-21), institutional investors slowly increased overseas stock investment in 2023 after 2022<sup>’</sup>s drop, and retail investors returned to a slight buying position in early 2024 after 2023<sup>’</sup>s withdrawal, but their levels remained anemic (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Korea locals' investment in foreign stocks
![](images/5b6772bc23590554e3b3be3595941139c0ab456577ba9da57d71c345a87b79b7.jpg)

FDI has also been a major channel for locals' overseas investment since before the pandemic (Figure 6), driven by institutional investors diversifying into alternative assets (e.g., private capital, real estate) via overseas special purpose companies, and non-financial corporations expanding production abroad in response to ongoing supply chain reshuffles. In 2021-22, outward FDI almost doubled. But it fell back below pre-pandemic levels in 2023 with monetary conditions turning to headwinds. We expect net FDI outflows to stabilize and see a modest recovery in 2024.

![](images/89030e185be4d15cf94fbb80a9f2e7e24063f6f5539e3bb6f8a5c6e5a5b438f8.jpg)

Foreigners<sup>’</sup> investment in local markets could also increase in 2024, counterbalancing some of locals<sup>’</sup> outflows (Figure 6). Foreigners<sup>’</sup> investment in local stocks, which turned positive in 2023 after three years of outflows, is anticipated to rise modestly further in 2024 with the current tech upcycle. Their investment in local bonds maintained net inflows since 2017, underpinned by public investors (e.g., central banks) comprising about 60% of total bond inflows. We expect stable bond inflows to persist in 2024, with a possible support from expectations for inclusion of KRW bonds in WGBI (World Government Bond Index).

## United States

• Core PCE inflation was solid in January

• January reports show pullback in real consumption but mixed news overall

• Real GDP growth forecast for 1Q raised to 2.25%

● Expect strong nonfarm job growth again in February (forecast: 200,000 with unemployment rate at 3.7%)

The PCE price data were the focus for many heading into the past week. The January data were uneventful in the sense that they came out pretty close to consensus expectations, but they were noteworthy in that they showed a firm month for core (and “supercore<sup>”</sup>) inflation that raises questions about the underlying trajectory for consumer prices. While the 0.4% increase in the core PCE price index in January marked the largest monthly gain in a year, the related year-ago inflation rate continued to moderate (edging down to 2.8%oya) and we believe that inflation will keep softening over time through some uneven monthly reports.

We also have been receiving a variety of GDP source reports for January. Real consumer spending pulled back 0.1% in the month after strong gains in earlier recent months, and more broadly the news looks consistent with GDP growth moderating between 4Q and 1Q. But this slowdown is looking less significant than we had previously been anticipating, and we raised our forecast for 1Q real GDP growth from 1.75% to 2.25% saar this week.

The main economic release next week will be the February employment report. While we expect clear slowing in job growth relative to January, we still expect solid results across many of the report<sup>’</sup>s key details given signals that the labor market remains strong. We forecast that nonfarm employment increased 200,000 in February while the unemployment rate held at 3.7%. We also believe the average workweek climbed back to 34.3 hours in February (after dropping in January) and that average hourly earnings rose a modest 0.2% (following a jump in January). The January JOLTS report also will provide some updated information on the labor market.

Fed chair Powell is set to testify before Congress on Wednesday and Thursday. We don<sup>’</sup>t expect him to stray much from recent messaging; he likely will convey that the FOMC plans to start lowering rates at some point but that the Committee wants more confidence that inflation is moderating before these cuts occur. The Fed<sup>’</sup>s recent monetary policy report (already released ahead of the upcoming testimony) indicated that “[t]he Committee does not expect it will be appropriate to reduce the target range until it has gained greater confidence that inflation is moving sustainably toward 2 percent.<sup>”</sup>

Apart from Powell<sup>’</sup>s testimony, we also will continue to monitor legislative developments in Washington, DC. Congress passed a bill to prevent a partial government shutdown from starting this week, although now funding agreements will still need to be passed to prevent a shutdown of some government agencies on March 8 and others on March 22.

## A firm month for core inflation

The firm core CPI print for January was followed by a firm reading on core PCE inflation for the month. The headline PCE price index rose 0.3% in January while the core index rose 0.4%. This monthly increase in the core was the largest monthly gain in a year and came largely due to firmness in the core services ex. housing aggregate—prices for this “supercore<sup>”</sup> measure jumped 0.6% in January, the largest monthly gain in about two years. We think that the firm January inflation data will prove to be largely noise along a softer trend, particularly if we are right that the economy is slowing and that labor markets will become less tight over time. While three-month run rates for the main PCE price measures have picked up lately, the related year-ago prints have continued their recent moderating trend, with the headline up 2.4%oya in January and the core price index rising 2.8%oya (Figure 1).

Figure 1: PCE price measures
%ch, saar, over specified number of months
![](images/9d2c81233eaaf7862ee370c362585e528b3c6c781a5582949fe9a9f02dc30ef4.jpg)

## 1Q GDP forecast revised up

The BEA revised its estimate of 4Q real GDP growth down from 3.3% to 3.2% this week. Despite this downward revision (that was a modest downside surprise), overall growth in 4Q was still solid and some key details related to final demand were revised up. The inventory change in 4Q also was revised down, which is beneficial as we think about 1Q growth. With the updated news on the 4Q data and additional January GDP source data released this month including data on real consumer spending and business spending on equipment, we now think the real GDP growth in 1Q will be 2.25% saar (up from our prior estimate of 1.75%).

Starting with the consumers, the PCE report showed a 1.0% jump in nominal income in January that was above expectations and supported by the annual Cost of Living Adjustment and an increase in Affordable Care Act enrollments. Apart from these likely one-off factors, employee compensation increased by a solid, though unspectacular, 0.4% in January. There was also a large increase in taxes reported for January, so disposable income rose 0.3% even with nominal income jumping by much more that month. And real consumer spending ticked down 0.1% in January even with the nominal income growth in January. The softening in January spending came after solid gains in earlier recent months, and the recent trajectory for the consumption data looks better than we had been anticipating going into the January report (helped by an upward revision to December).

Figure 2: Core capital goods orders and shipments
![](images/2d3aec0a9c2971a112e5910d0499faeadfd1c8c6ba99cdf21d06bf6d391a1174.jpg)

Similarly, on the business spending side, data we received this week highlighted stronger-than-expected equipment spending for the beginning of the quarter, despite overall weakness. For instance, new orders for durable goods tumbled 6.1% in January, led by weakness in the aircraft data, but orders for the important core capital goods grouping (which excludes defense and aircraft), inched up 0.1% in January after dropping 0.6% in December (December was revised down), surprising us on the upside (Figure 2). Core capital goods shipments, meanwhile, posted solid growth in January, with an 0.8% increase that month following a 0.1% move up in December. These data and related figures on foreign trade imply a better profile for equipment spending in 1Q than we previously had been anticipating.

Separately, construction spending declined 0.2% in January, although this outcome was stronger than our more downbeat forecast and revisions to earlier figures were favorable. The construction data are often noisy and severe weather may have dampened the January results, so we don<sup>’</sup>t want to read too much into the January weakening. And even with soft January data, we still think that real residential investment is on track for strong growth in 1Q, with support coming from spending on new housing construction.

## Consumers sour a bit in February

Data on consumer sentiment/confidence reported this week pointed to some weakening in February. The Conference Board reported that its consumer confidence index declined from 110.9 to 106.7 in February (Figure 3). The February decline undid only a portion of the 11.8pt increase in the confidence index reported over the prior three months, but now it looks like this gauge of consumer attitudes has been drifting roughly sideways (or maybe modestly higher) lately through some ups and downs in the data.

Figure 3: CB and University of Michigan consumer surveys
![](images/8ee1912d165f7ff910e0f177187e4dd6a239d1b5ed3e624cbd1e33f1050e7955.jpg)
Source: Conference Board, University of Michigan, J.P. Morgan

The University of Michigan consumer sentiment index was also somewhat disappointing this week. The index was revised down from 79.6 to 76.9 between the preliminary and final February reports. This was a pretty large downward revision, and the index now shows some weakening in sentiment relative to the 79.0 reported for January (the preliminary February print showed firming relative to January). Despite this latest cooling, there still has been solid improvement on net for this measure in recent months through some ups and downs in the monthly prints.

The University of Michigan index likely has been supported by rising equity markets in recent months. Labor market strength also likely has helped, although the Conference Board survey places more explicit emphasis on the labor market than the University of Michigan survey. In terms of labor market news, jobless claims filings have moved up somewhat lately but generally have remained low, looking consistent with labor market strength. Similarly, the Conference Board survey<sup>’</sup>s labor market differential has come down somewhat but still looks solid.

The University of Michigan survey's main inflation expectation measures were unrevised between the preliminary and final February reports, with the median one-year-ahead expectation at 3.0% (up from 2.9% in January) and the median five-year-ahead expectation at 2.9% (unchanged from January).
