We believe the nominal trade balance widened from -\$62.2bn in December to -\$64.1bn in January, with imports increasing 1.0% and exports rising 0.4%. The advance trade report already showed the nominal goods deficit widening in January with imports rising 1.1% and exports up 0.2%. Factoring in price data released separately, we believe real goods imports rose 0.6% in January while real goods exports were unchanged. For the nominal services data, we expect the growth trend to continue to be stronger for exports than imports, and we estimate that exports increased 0.9% in January while imports rose 0.3%.

<table><tr><td>Thu Productivity and costs Mar 7 8:30am Productivity %oya</td><td>Nonfarm business sector, %q/q, saar, unless noted</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="10"></td><td></td><td>Rev</td><td>Prel</td><td>Rev</td></tr><tr><td>4.9</td><td>3Q23</td><td>4Q23</td><td>4Q23</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>4.9</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.1</td></tr><tr><td>2.3</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>Output</td><td>5.8 5.8</td><td>3.7</td><td>3.5</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>3.1 3.1</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>Hourly compensation</td><td>3.8 4.6</td><td>3.7</td><td>3.8</td></tr><tr><td>%oya Unit labor costs</td><td>4.0 4.2</td><td>5.0</td><td>5.3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>-1.1 -0.3 1.7</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>1.9</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>%oya Hours %oya</td><td>0.9 0.8</td><td>0.9 0.8</td><td>0.4 0.6</td><td>0.4 0.6</td></tr></table>

We estimate that nonfarm productivity growth in 4Q will be revised down from 3.2% to 3.1% based on the downward revision to output growth during the quarter already reported by the BEA. We expect more notable changes to come in the data on unit labor costs given upward revisions released by the BEA for related compensation figures (particularly in 3Q but also in 4Q). We believe that the 4Q change in unit labor costs will be revised up from 0.5% to 0.7% saar, with the related year-ago reading being revised from 2.3% to 2.6%.

We forecast that nonfarm employment increased 200,000 in February while the unemployment rate held at 3.7%. Job growth was robust in January, with the 353,000 jobs added that month marking one of the largest monthly job gains in over a year. We believe that this strong January job growth could have been flattered by favorable seasonal adjustment that anticipated steep job cuts before the seasonal adjustment and this is one of the reasons we expect job growth to slow significantly in February relative to January (the increase in jobless claims filings between the reference weeks for the January and February reports is another). But even though we believe job growth will cool in February, we still see a number of signs that the labor market remains strong and we expect a still-solid amount of jobs to be added that month; filings for unemployment insurance generally have stayed low lately and consumer responses about the availability of jobs have remained fairly upbeat. We also think that the public sector likely continued to make a decent-sized contribution to employment growth in February, with governments adding another 40,000 jobs that month. For the private sector, we look for 160,000 jobs to be added in February with goods industries generating 35,000 and the services sector accounting for 125,000.

While job growth was strong in January, there were also signs of adverse weather impacting the data for the month. In the household survey, an above-average amount of people were classified as employed but not at work because of the weather. And the establishment survey showed a drop in the average workweek along with a surge in average hourly earnings, and this combination looks consistent with weather limiting the amount of hours worked around the reference period for the employment data and salaried workers seeing their average wage per hour jump as a result. We look for some reversal of these January changes to take place in February, with the average workweek increasing from 34.1 to 34.3 hours and average hourly earnings rising a modest 0.2% in February. With our expectations for job growth and the workweek, we believe the aggregate hours index jumped 0.7% in February.

For the household survey, we also expect solid employment growth in February to roughly keep up with population

growth. We believe the unemployment rate held at 3.7% between January and February while the participation rate stayed at 62.5%.

## New home sales

<table><tr><td></td><td>Nov</td><td></td><td>Dec</td><td></td><td>Jan</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total (000s,saar)</td><td>615</td><td>607</td><td>664</td><td>651</td><td>680</td><td>661</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>9.0</td><td>-9.4</td><td>8.0</td><td>7.2</td><td>2.4</td><td>1.5</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>2.4</td><td></td><td>6.4</td><td>4.3</td><td>4.8</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply</td><td>8.8</td><td>8.9</td><td>8.2</td><td>8.3</td><td></td><td>8.3</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>7.9</td><td>-7.4</td><td>-13.8</td><td></td><td></td><td>-2.6</td></tr></table>

New single-family home sales increased 1.5% to 661,000 saar in January. The January figures were below expectations, in part because of downward revisions to earlier prints, but the data still show back-to-back monthly increases in sales across December and January. Several other home sale indicators also have firmed recently and it looks like housing activity has responded favorably to recent declines in mortgage rates. An increased availability of homes for sale could also help boost sales, and we have seen inventories of new homes trend higher lately (in seasonally adjusted terms, the inventory of new homes available for sale with completed construction almost matched its highest level in over a decade in January). Increased inventory also could weigh on prices, and the median new home sale price has trend lower lately (-2.6% oya as of January). This gauge of house prices does not control for changes in the mix of sales so it may not be particularly reliable, and the average new home sale price—another imperfect price metric—jumped in January despite the softer trend for the median price measure.

## Durable goods (Feb 27)

<table><tr><td colspan="2">%m/m, sa</td><td rowspan="2">Dec</td><td rowspan="2">Jan</td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Nov</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>5.4</td><td>-0.0 -0.3</td><td>5.2</td><td>-6.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex transportation</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.5 -0.1</td><td>-0.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>0.9</td><td>-0.2 -0.6</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>1.0</td><td>-0.3 -0.6</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.9</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.0 0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.4 0.3</td><td></td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

New orders for durable goods tumbled 6.1% in January. This big drop was largely tied to a 52% plunge in aircraft orders which was roughly expected, and the aircraft data can be noisy and heavily influenced by sector-specific issues (aircraft shipments also fell in January, but by a much less severe amount). Away from aircraft, several key details in the report were much better than the headline reading, although they

were not particularly great. For the important core capital goods grouping (which excludes defense and aircraft), orders inched up 0.1% in January after dropping 0.6% in December (December was revised down). Core capital goods orders were up 0.1% oya in January, and these generally have been drifting very modestly higher over the past year through some noise in the monthly prints. But related prices likely have increased by more lately, so the volume data (not included in the durable goods report) probably have been much weaker than the nominal figures. Core capital goods shipments, meanwhile, posted solid growth in January, with a 0.8% increase that month following a 0.1% move up in December. An increase in prices likely flattered the nominal January data, but it still looks like core capital goods shipments moved higher that month in volume terms.

## Consumer confidence (Feb 27)

The Conference Board consumer confidence index declined from 110.9 to 106.7 in February. This disappointed expectations in both that we and the consensus had expected some modest firming during the month and also that the January data were revised lower. The February decline undid only a portion of the 11.8-pt increase in the confidence index reported over the prior three months, but now it looks like this gauge of consumer attitudes has been drifting roughly sideways (or maybe modestly higher) lately through some ups and downs in the data. The Conference Board survey also showed some weakening in its labor market differential in February along with a downward revision reported for January. But even though this measure declined from 31.7 in January to 27.8 in February, this latest figure still looks indicative of labor market strength/tightness.

The BEA revised its estimate of 4Q real GDP growth down from 3.3% to 3.2%. Despite this downward revision (that was a modest downside surprise), overall growth in 4Q was still solid and some key details related to final demand were revised up. The inventory change in 4Q also was revised down, which is beneficial as we think about 1Q growth.

In the updated 4Q data, real final sales increased 3.5% saar in 4Q (previously: 3.2%) and real domestic final sales rose 3.1% saar (previously: 2.7%) and most of the major underlying categories were revised higher (equipment spending was the main exception, with the 4Q change revised down from +1.0% to -1.7% saar). The updated GDP data also show inventories subtracting 0.3%-pts from GDP growth in 4Q after the earlier report had shown a positive 0.1%-pt contribution.

The main PCE price indexes for 4Q were revised up modestly, with the GDP deflator now up 1.6% saar that quarter (previously: 1.5%), the PCE price index increasing 1.8% saar (previously: 1.7%), and the core PCE deflator up 2.1% saar (previously: 2.0%). The revisions were small enough that the related year-ago rates for the GDP price index and the core PCE deflator were unrevised after rounding (at 2.6% oya and 3.2% oya, respectively), although the headline PCE price index is now shown to be up 2.8% oya in 4Q (previously: 2.7% oya).

## Personal income

<table><tr><td colspan="2">%m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td rowspan="2"></td><td colspan="3">Dec</td><td rowspan="2">Jan</td></tr><tr><td>Personal income</td><td>Nov -0.4</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Wages &amp; salaries</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.2</td><td>1.0 0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Consumption</td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Real consumption</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>PCE price index</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Core</td><td>0.06 0.09</td><td></td><td>0.17 0.14</td><td></td><td>0.47</td><td>0.42</td></tr><tr><td>Mkt-Based Core</td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td></td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Core (%oya)</td><td>3.2</td><td></td><td>2.9</td><td></td><td>29</td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td>Mkt-Based Core (%oya)</td><td>3.2</td><td></td><td>3.0</td><td></td><td></td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td>Saving rate</td><td>4.1</td><td></td><td>3.7</td><td></td><td>3.9</td><td>3.8</td></tr></table>

The January PCE price changes were in line with the consensus expectation (although somewhat below our forecasts). The headline PCE price index rose 0.3% on the month and was up 2.4% oya. The core PCE price index, meanwhile, increased 0.4% (0.416% to three decimals) and was up 2.8% oya. Much of the strength in the January data came from the super-core grouping (core services ex. housing) and the PCE price index for this aggregate jumped 0.6% in January, marking the largest monthly gain since December 2021; this measure is now reported up 3.5% oya. Despite these generally strong results for the January price changes, the year-ago inflation rates for the three aforementioned aggregates are still down from where they were six months ago (and down even more from where they were 12 months ago) and we continue to think that the broader underlying trend for inflation will be moderating over time.

The PCE report also showed a 1.0% jump in nominal income in January that was above expectations and supported by the annual Cost of Living Adjustment and an increase in Affordable Care Act enrollments. Apart from these likely one-off factors, employee compensation increased by a solid, though unspectacular, 0.4% in January. There was also a large increase in taxes reported for January, so disposable income rose 0.3% even with nominal income jumping by much more that month. And real consumer spending ticked down 0.1% in January even with the nominal income growth in January. The softening in January spending came after solid gains in earlier recent months, and the recent trajectory for the consumption data looks better than we had been anticipating going into the January report (helped by an upward revision to December).

It still looks like there has been some firming in housing activity lately which likely is a response to declines in mortgage rates, but according to the pending home sales data, this pickup is now less robust than was previously evident and also may end up being pretty short-lived. ## Construction spending (Mar 1)

<table><tr><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>Nov</td><td></td><td>Dec</td><td></td><td>Jan</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Nominal</td><td>-0.9</td><td>1.2</td><td>0.9</td><td>1.1</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Private</td><td>1.1</td><td></td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.8</td><td>-0.6</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Residential</td><td>1.0</td><td>-0.4</td><td>1.4</td><td></td><td>0.8</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Nonresidential</td><td>44</td><td>3.0</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Public</td><td>-0.5</td><td>1.3</td><td>1.3</td><td>2.0</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.9</td></tr></table>

Nominal construction spending declined 0.2% in January. Revisions to earlier months were positive and enough to push our tracking estimate of 4Q real GDP growth up from 3.2% to 3.4% saar. The decline in spending in January was largely tied to the public sector (-0.9%) although private nonresidential spending and private residential spending also had soft months (-0.1% and +0.2%, respectively). The data can be noisy from month to month and we think adverse weather may have negatively impacted the January data. So while January showed some weakening in the trend for the construction spending data, we don<sup>’</sup>t want to take too much signal from this latest monthly print.

## Focus: Housing set up for short-lived strong growth in 1Q

Many housing indicators continue to demonstrate their sensitivity to mortgage rates. The decline in mortgage rates from late October to late December seems to have generated some firming in housing activity late last year and early this year, but the more recent move up in rates looks to have dampened certain types of activity once again (Figure 1). Of course, many factors beyond just mortgage rates can influence housing activity, and the various housing measures that we track do not always move in similar manners. We see signs that the firming in housing measures, particularly those related to home sales, may prove to be short-lived given the recent increase in rates. That said, we still think that real residential investment is set up for strong growth on average in 1Q given the firming seen late last year and early this year that came off of very low levels of activity. We think that real residential investment will jump about 7% saar in 1Q, with this increase followed by much softer growth figures in subsequent quarters.

Figure 1: Mortgage rates and purchase applications
![](images/1fcaee77b9c4ba5aedcfa186b1f4289716c1e5010637d36bb7dfeb5a6f6a53d0.jpg)

Changes in mortgage purchase application volumes have been negatively correlated with changes in mortgage rates in recent years. Purchase applications generally trended up from their recent low in late October to a recent high in mid-January, but then fell in the five straight weeks reported since then (Figure 1). Similarly, pending home sales (which are counted when contracts are signed and lead the existing home sales data, which are counted when transactions are completed) perked up in December before stepping down again in January (not shown). We have yet to see a corresponding move down in new and existing home sales (through January figures) but believe that there will be some cooling in activity to come given the recent weakening in these other related measures. That said, home sales likely will jump overall in 1Q given the strong start to the quarter relative to 4Q, and this should generate strong growth in brokers<sup>’</sup> commissions that factor into the BEA<sup>’</sup>s estimate of residential investment—the rate of home sales in January was almost 13% higher than the 4Q average (annualized), even though the level of sales was still low early this year (Figure 2). Even if sales cool down in the remainder of the first quarter, we still expect solid growth for 1Q relative to 4Q.

Figure 2: New and existing home sales
Mn, saar, new home sales are SF only, existing home sales are SF and MF
![](images/e625710334e44c2cde71b50f098e83c1ca470690a7d0573ae926b2f531e446be.jpg)

While interest rates likely matter for housing construction, we think construction in recent years has been largely tied to limits in available inventory and the related spending data have not shown much sensitivity to short-term fluctuations in mortgage rates. Construction on new houses in January was about 8% above the 4Q average (annualized), setting the stage for strong growth in 1Q (Figure 3). There continue to be a large amount of houses under construction which should generate future spending, although the number of houses under construction has flattened out lately.

Figure 3: New housing constr. spending and houses under constr.
![](images/c59bdb1808b85336bf4129cf095754a08f650e5b79c5102f697d5644073d0826.jpg)

Some other measures have not looked as favorable as the aforementioned details lately, including separate data signaling a recent downward trend for spending on home improvements (not shown), but we still think that real residential investment is on track for solid growth in 1Q. Momentum likely will be weaker late in the current quarter, and we look for more modest growth in the near-term as we think beyond 1Q.

## Euro Area

• February flash HICP delivered a significant upside surprise

• Services inflation looks very sticky with an increase of 0.5%m/m, sa,

This could nudge ECB policy statement in a more hawkish direction

• Labor market remains solid

Next week, the ECB is almost certain to leave rates unchanged. The focus will instead by on the new staff projections and on the broader rhetoric. In our view, the staff forecasts will show a bit of progress toward meeting the inflation objective, but mainly via the effect of a sharply lower gas and electricity price assumption on headline inflation. In contrast, incoming information on wages and unit labor costs have been broadly in line with the ECB staff<sup>’</sup>s December forecast. However, these projections will not include the February flash HICP report, which was strong, with services prices rising 0.5%m/m, sa. That could nudge the policy statement in a more hawkish direction and keep the ECB firmly in datawatching mode for now.

In terms of our own call, we had in mid-January pulled the first cut forward from September to June, but we noted at the time that a June-to-September timeframe still felt appropriate to us. Hence, we discounted the likelihood of earlier moves. We still feel that way and the February flash HICP reinforces this. In fact, there is pressure on upcoming inflation prints to give some reassurance about the larger monthly gain in services prices in February - the full details of the January final report will be in focus prior to that data.

## A sticky upside HICP surprise

The flash HICP report for February delivered a significant upside surprise (Figure 1). Core inflation was affected significantly by weights distortions in 2H23 and the January prints can be noisy. The increase of 0.32%m/m, sa, in core prices in January had looked firm, but included the German VAT increase for restaurant meals and a record jump in insurance prices. Hence, arguably, it was softer than it appeared. It is less clear if there were any distortions in February, which makes the increase of 0.35%m/m, sa, more significant. In addition, it reflected a steady gain in core goods prices of 0.10%m/m, sa, and a very large increase in services prices of 0.49%m/m, sa. The latter is a concern as it captures domestic inflation and labor cost pressures.

Figure 1: Euro area HICP core prices
![](images/ac146ebb56d11df28c33a58f9300a38ded44044dc455960512af1fc066f1f748.jpg)

It is risky to put too much weight on a single print, especially when very few details are available. But, two other aspects add to the overall concern. First, the print included a large fall in Belgian core goods inflation that was not shown by its national CPI data. Second, the annual rate was lower on the ECB<sup>’</sup>s seasonally adjusted data than on Eurostat<sup>’</sup>s non-seasonally adjusted data. Often such gaps close by the Eurostat data moving towards the ECB data. But, if some of the 7bp gap closes in the other direction it would add to the strength of the February print.

For now, we fully incorporate the upside surprise into our inflation forecast (Figure 2). The effect is to widen the gap between core goods inflation, which has slowed substantially to a modest pace, and services inflation, which is expected to slow much more slowly. But we will also cross-check this when we get the full details from the final report.

Figure 2: Euro area core inflation
%oya, dotted line shows ECB inflation target, shaded is J.P. Morgan fcst
![](images/30d0dfed67acf37a27276f8955f6b74002b06708772e6960fc0add5e95b5999c.jpg)

## ECB staff to show some limited progress

Compared to the December projections, the new staff forecasts will incorporate lower assumptions on interest rates (by 30bp) and gas and electricity prices (by over 30%). These are positive for growth, but incoming data have continued to undershoot the staff<sup>’</sup>s forecast. As a result, we think that the pickup in growth will be delayed yet again (Table 1), thereby lowering only the 2024 forecast. A pickup will still be expected as the outlook for real household incomes remains solid, corporates have been resilient to the recent sluggishness of

GDP and the global trade backdrop is likely still expected to improve. These factors suggest that conditions for a growth pickup remain in place. This will keep unemployment in the forecast at historically low levels.

Table 1: ECB staff forecast for growth and inflation
<table><tr><td></td><td></td><td>GDP</td><td></td><td></td><td>HICP</td><td></td><td>Core HICP</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>2024</td><td>2025</td><td>2026</td><td>2024</td><td>2025</td><td>2026</td><td>2024</td><td>2025</td><td>2026</td></tr><tr><td>Mar 22</td><td>1.6</td><td></td><td></td><td>1.9</td><td>一</td><td></td><td>1.9</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jun 22</td><td>2.1</td><td></td><td></td><td>2.1</td><td></td><td></td><td>2.3</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Sep 22</td><td>1.9</td><td></td><td></td><td>2.3</td><td></td><td></td><td>2.3</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Dec 22</td><td>1.9</td><td>1.8</td><td></td><td>3.4</td><td>2.3</td><td></td><td>2.8</td><td>2.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Mar 23</td><td>1.6</td><td>1.6</td><td></td><td>2.9</td><td>2.1</td><td></td><td>2.5</td><td>2.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jun 23</td><td>1.5</td><td>1.6</td><td></td><td>3.0</td><td>2.2</td><td></td><td>3.0</td><td>2.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Sep 23</td><td>1.0</td><td>1.5</td><td></td><td>3.2</td><td>2.1</td><td></td><td>2.9</td><td>2.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Dec 23</td><td>0.8</td><td>1.5</td><td>1.5</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.1</td><td>1.9</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.1</td></tr><tr><td>Mar 24</td><td>0.6</td><td>1.5</td><td>1.6</td><td>2.4</td><td>1.8</td><td>1.8</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.2</td><td>2.0</td></tr></table>

Source: ECB, J.P.Morgan.

On inflation, the incoming data have undershot the ECB staff<sup>’</sup>s December projection, although more on headline than on core. For 1Q24, the ECB staff expected 2.9%oya for headline and 3.1%oya for core. Data through January were tracking closer to 2.6%oya on headline and 3.0%oya on core, but this precedes the February flash reports.

In terms of the drivers, negotiated pay growth looks sticky at around 4.5%oya, while compensation per employee and unit labor costs are likely slowing in line with the December projections. But, the delayed recovery in GDP does keep the bias on unit labor costs on the upside. This is because sluggish GDP growth is not deterring corporates from hiring and hence keeps measured labor productivity under pressure. At the same time, the sharp fall in gas and electricity prices may also weigh on core inflation via indirect effects. Overall, we think the staff may cut its core inflation forecast by 0.1%-pt for 2025 and 2026. But, at 2.2%oya, the 2025 forecast would still be above target and a number of policymakers have warned against shifting the focus to 2026. Hence, the new staff forecasts will only suggest some limited progress compared to the December forecast.

## Wrapping up other data

The Euro area economy continues to grow sluggishly, but the incoming labor market data remain solid. The Euro area unemployment rate was revised a tenth higher for prior months, but January showed a 0.1%-pt decline to 6.4%m/m (Figure 3). The number of unemployed also continued to fall by another 34,000. This improvement is skewed towards Italy and Spain, but the German report for February was also decent, especially because it showed a solid increase in employment (Figure 4). Overall, labor markets are easing at the margin as vacancies are steadily declining and the workweek has also shortened. However, considering the weakness in growth, that employment is still rising is remarkable.

Figure 3: Euro area unemployment
![](images/5e3a90bc159eeae070e4f5edd13b189c7f16a0ab34a78e257c389695abf6518c.jpg)

Figure 4: German employment
![](images/6067fbc134df6697f7dac5b7f1783b578fb9515d7e0337fe39aae0652874715c.jpg)
Source: Bundesbank, J.P. Morgan

Other data were more mixed this week. Euro area economic sentiment declined 0.7pt to 95.4 in February. At face value this goes against the message of the PMI, which had increased by 1pt in February. In addition, the manufacturing PMI was revised up around 0.5-pt in this week<sup>’</sup>s final report, which reinforces the sense of improvement. We note however that economic sentiment is still higher than the PMI and that we put more weight on the PMI when tracking GDP growth.

On the consumer side, German retail sales were revised significantly higher for 4Q23. At one point, that was tracking a decline of 2%q/q saar, but this has improved to a gain of around 0.5%q/q saar. At the same time, the January report got 1Q24 off to a weak start. But, the German data are noisy and prone to revision and therefore we are not drawing too many conclusions from this just yet. We note also that overall consumer spending has increased in 3Q23 in the Euro area and that national data are pointing to a similar increase again in 4Q23. Hence, our forecast of an improvement in consumer spending is tracking, even though this is hard to monitor with the monthly data.

The Euro area composite PMI improved significantly in February, despite a sharp deterioration in German manufacturing. The manufacturing PMI has been revised up by around 0.5pt, which could also modestly lift the composite. But, even if there are no revisions, the flash composite PMI showed a 1pt improvement, which raised the signal for GDP growth to a 0.4%ar. The PMI needs to improve further to validate our 2Q24 forecast, but it is now in the right ballpark for 1Q24.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Industrial production</td><td>Oct</td><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td><td>Jan</td></tr><tr><td>Fri</td><td>Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Mar 8</td><td>Industry incl constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-1.6</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>8:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>-3.4</td><td>-4.4</td><td>-3.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Industry ex constr (%m/m, sa)</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-1.2</td><td>1.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Manufacturing (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-1.5</td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Construction (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-1.3</td><td>-0.9</td><td>-3.4</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Energy (%m/m, sa)</td><td>9.8</td><td>6.5</td><td>4.7</td><td>1.0</td></tr></table>

German IP was weak in December. Adverse weather contributed to the sharp fall in construction output and this likely remained weak in January as the weather disruption index in the construction IFO remained high. Energy output continued to recover from low levels in December and may have improved further in January. It was unclear whether adverse weather in early December also contributed to the manufacturing weakness. The surveys suggest that the trend in manufacturing was still downwards in January, but the correlation between the surveys and IP has not been that tight recently. There has also been a larger divergence between car production in the IP data and the VDA in December, which adds to the uncertainty. The final point is that IP has not been a good indicator of the level of activity recorded in the GDP data for manufacturing. Therefore any outturn must be interpreted cautiously.

French IP increased significantly in the last two months of 2023. We think this increase will be partly offset by some payback at the start of the 2024. The PMI manufacturing output data rebounded significantly in February but remained at a low level, thus manufacturing production likely will remain weak in the first few months of the year.

German orders surged in December, largely due to a largescale aircraft orders. Excluding bulk orders, there was a decline of 2.2%m/m in December and a 10%q/q saar decline in 4Q23 overall. January should see sharp payback in total orders. Ex bulk orders should improve though after the large decline in December. However, the surveys still signal a weak underlying trend with contraction at around a 5%ar.

The decline was driven by a broad-based move down in business sentiment (manufacturing, services, retail and construction), while consumer sentiment increased on the back of significant real wage gains. Despite the February decline, the signal of the EC sentiment survey remains stronger than the signal of the composite output PMI, but we put more weight on the latter since it correlates better with GDP growth. The signal of the PMI survey overall is broadly aligned with our 0.5% ar 1Q GDP forecast. One interesting aspect in the EC sentiment survey is the country breakdown (not fully available at the time of the flash PMI release). Among the main economies of the region, these data continue to show that sentiment is weakest in Germany and strongest in Italy and Spain.

In the German labor market report, the pickup in employment is noteworthy, with 54,000 jobs created in January or 0.1%m/ m. A few months ago, the data had ground to a halt and are now picking up towards a 1%ar. This is also not entirely inconsistent with the business surveys: the output index in the German composite PMI fell in February, but the employment index rose further. At the same time, the recent improvement in vacancies has halted again, with a 7,000 decline in February, and unemployment rose by 12,000 in February. Applications for and usage of the short-time work subsidy scheme have shown no meaningful change recently. So, despite some mixed aspects, the labour market data continue to be very resilient, given the weakness in overall growth. High levels of labour shortages, weakness in the workweek and decent corporate positions may be contributing to this.

<sup>1.</sup> Loans to nonbank private sector, adjusted for securitization

The M3 report was softer for January, but the smoother threemonth change still shows a modest improvement. Bank lending to households rose by €5bn, which is the largest gain in 10 months. The more volatile loans to non-financial corporates showed a decline of €12bn, reversing only some of the recent gains. Overall, lending to the real economy fell by €7bn, which nudged the three-month average down to +€9bn. Looked at together with the recent ECB Bank Lending Survey, the intensity of the weakness in bank lending is abating, but not yet turning to a meaningful pickup. On the liability side of bank<sup>’</sup>s balance sheets, money aggregates weakened in January, but there is also still evidence of portfolio shifts from the liquid M1 balances into longer-term deposits that currently earn more interest. Hence, the signal from real M1 to the PMI is likely still distorted by this.

## Japan

• There has been a divergence between strong market momentum and weak economic activity data

In Jan, retail sales recovered from the Dec fall, and IP marked the largest monthly fall since May 2020

• With weak activity data, we revise our 1Q GDP forecasts down to 0.2%q/q, saar from 0.8%

• We continue to expect the BoJ to start normalization with BS policy rather than a rate hike

While the market continues to expect the BoJ to remove NIRP in upcoming meetings as a preset course, we continue to observe a large gap between the strong market momentum and softening economic activity. This week<sup>’</sup>s January activity data showed weak consumer spending and a sharp decline in production at the start of the year. Contrary to the trend improvement in consumer sentiment since 4Q23 backed by falling headline inflation, real consumption slowed towards the end of last year. There are still no clear signs of recovery after the turn of the year. Although the upcoming spring wage negotiation should point to higher wage growth than last year, we expect real wages to continue to be depressed until 2Q, when the negotiated wage increases will actually be paid. We thus push back the timing of the consumption recovery to 2Q, revising down our 1Q GDP-based real consumption growth forecasts from 0.8%q/q, saar, to 0.2%.

Production also fell sharply in January, more than market expectations. Although a decline in production was widely expected due to the Noto earthquake and the auto plants shutdown by one of the major automakers, the actual decline was larger and more widespread than expected. Although these temporary factors are expected to gradually fade from February, manufacturers<sup>’</sup> production forecasts indicate that the recovery will proceed only at a gradual pace. These suggest production to remain anemic throughout 1Q, likely marking the largest decline since the start of the pandemic. We now expect industrial production to plunge 18.5%q/q, saar, in 1Q.

Next week<sup>’</sup>s key data releases are the 4Q23 MoF Corporate Survey, February Tokyo CPI, January wages and BoJ Consumption Activity Index. In the Tokyo CPI, we expect headline measures to jump, reflecting higher electricity prices. What is more important in predicting BoJ policy is price momentum in services. We expect wage momentum to remain strong, but also expect weak real consumption, as suggested by this week<sup>’</sup>s retail sales. If we are right, the data next week will challenge the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s economic assessment, as Governor Ueda earlier stated that “We have received various side evidence that consumption during the year-end and New Year holidays was strong<sup>”</sup>.

We believe that the BoJ will start gradual policy normalization after confirming the wage increases at the upcoming spring wage negotiations. However, given the combination of strong market momentum and a temporary slump in domestic demand, we still believe the adjustments to balance sheet policy, such as changes in asset purchase programs, will take precedence over a rate hike at the start of policy normalization. The BoJ likely will wait until domestic demand is recovering before a rate hike, which we expect to happen in midyear.

## Production plunged at the start of the year

Industrial production came out weaker than expected (JPM: -3.0%, Consensus: -6.8%), plunging 7.5%m/m, sa, in January (Figure 1). The production weakness was widely expected, with the Noto earthquake and an announcement of auto plants shutdown of a large auto maker, but the actual decline was even larger and broader-based. Indeed, except for a small rise in transportation machinery (excluding autos), all industries marked monthly declines. Among them, autos recorded the largest decline, plummeting 17.8%; this was mainly due to one of the major automakers suspending operations at all plants due to inspection fraud (Figure 2). This may have had some spillover to other industries, such as electronics and electrical & IT machinery production, which also fell 5.8%, and 8.3%, respectively. And this reversed the recent trend upswings in tech production, albeit only for a month.

Figure 1: Manufacturing output breadth
![](images/53f4f5cf14c4f9b176301e6d7722e9661319c873f5f0247ad0b675f907fd183f.jpg)

Manufacturers project a recovery in production in February and March, of 4.8% and 2.0%, respectively. But METI<sup>’</sup>s projection, adjusting for statistics bias, points to only a modest 0.8% gain in February. The automaker has already announced it will resume plant operations from February, but the expected recovery in production appears to remain slow. Also, production disruptions caused by the Noto earthquake may last longer than initially expected. We expect IP to fall into a sharp contraction in 1Q24, falling 18.5%q/q, saar, after a 5.1% gain in 4Q23.

Figure 2: Manufacturing output by sector 2013=100, sa
![](images/fcbc2571d09a710066af4fd907884f59675d741056c9447a8b3d53b1c5ffebcb.jpg)

## Consumption to remain soft in 1Q

Retail sales rose 0.8%m/m, sa, in January, modestly softer than our expectation of a 1.0% rise (Figure 3). The January gain was only a modest recovery from the December plunge of -2.6%. Despite the price effects caused by sticky inflation, retail sales have been trending down lately, after hitting a peak in September 2023. We think the recent trend suggests softening consumption activity due to persistent income drags.

Figure 3: Retail sales and consumer sentiment
![](images/ca89ba7dfa98c56d358579f9441e64235dd9689b3d8da4b79e89d02880aa3120.jpg)

Consumer sentiment beat expectations in February, rising 1.1pt to 39.1. Consumer sentiment has been trending up in recent months, after having hit the recent bottom of 35.2 in September. The recovery was broad-based among sub-indices, but the standard of living index rose the most in cumulative 5 months, reflecting the recent decline in headline inflation. Although the trend recovery in consumer sentiment has yet to feed into a recovery in consumer spending, we see this result as a positive signal for consumption ahead, and continue to expect consumption to recover along with a gradual fading of real income drags.

Reflecting the recent moderation in economic data, we revise our 1Q24 GDP-based real consumption forecast down to 0.2%q/q, saar, from a 0.8% rise. Although consumer sentiment is recovering, the persistent real income drags are likely to continue to hinder a consumption recovery in 1Q as the expected wage gain has yet to materialize. We still expect a consumption recovery once wage growth catches up with inflation, but expect this to happen starting from 2Q.

## CPI inflation slowed in January

The January nationwide CPI report showed a slowing in underlying inflation, but was firmer than expectations (both JPM and consensus) which were based on the surprise drop in the January Tokyo print released earlier (Figure 4). The global core measure (ex. food and energy) fell to 2.6%oya in January from 2.8% in December, but the moderation was attributable to the recent trend decline in core goods prices due to base effects. Core goods price inflation (ex. food and energy) has been trending down since April 2023, but still showed some stickiness, rising 0.3%m/m, sa, to 3.6%oya, down by 0.3%-pt from December.

Service price inflation remained flat at 2.2%oya, as moderations in communication and recreation charges were largely offset by the rises in transportation charges. While the overall services measure still remained solid, the January print showed some moderation in price momentum, with a softening in inflation for services outside of administrative controls.

The official core CPI (ex. only fresh food, de-facto headline) decelerated from 2.3%oya in December to 2.0% in January, due to the decline in electricity prices, which reflected lagged effects of lower imported fuel prices with around a six-month lag. However, the trend of weak energy prices will likely reverse, reflecting the rise in underlying electricity prices starting from February, as well as the end of the government<sup>’</sup>s energy subsidy programs from June/July.

Despite the January moderation, we continue to expect nationwide global core CPI to stay above 2% at least for a year ahead; the recent rise in wage momentum should lift services inflation. We also continue to think this will support the BoJ proceeding with policy normalization at a gradual pace. We remain skeptical that normalization will start with a policy rate hike, given the recent moderation in domestic demand.

Figure 4: Global core prices (ex. food and energy)
![](images/8345566799d86799ebdb3670966c9856cb9e61cba306f7a42bb50e5cb61a17df.jpg)

## Canada

• Bank of Canada expected to hold the policy rate next week

Bank needs more evidence that inflation is on a sustainable path to 2.0% target

• Growth in 2023 slows to 0.9%Q4/Q4

We expect the Bank of Canada to remain on hold at the monetary policy announcement next week. While 4Q23 real GDP growth was significantly above the Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast for flat growth, growth in 2023 on the whole was very close to its forecast after revisions. And the growth landscape still generally aligns with the Bank<sup>’</sup>s view that monetary policy has worked to decelerate activity. The economy eked out belowpotential growth of 0.9%Q4/Q4 in 2023 following 2.2% in 2022. The Bank<sup>’</sup>s baseline forecast is that growth will remain soft in 2024, matching our view. The Bank has declared that the economy is no longer in a state of excess demand. Indeed, the Bank judges that the economy has a modest amount of excess supply and that a widening output gap will continue to relieve inflationary pressures. On inflation the Bank will only have one more CPI report since the January meeting. The report was generally favorable but the Bank will need more evidence that inflation is on a sustainable path to the 2.0% target. Rebounding demand for services over the cycle and heated shelter costs have made services inflation stickier than goods inflation. As the labor market continues to converge toward balance and the consumer remains restrained by debt servicing costs this should allow services prices to continue to moderate. We think that core inflation (ex. food and energy) will ease from a 3.9% annualized pace in 2023 to 2.6% in 1H24 and 2.1% in 2H24 as core services inflation subsides and core goods inflation remains stable and subdued (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core CPIs
![](images/d4625c9c31debb7c9b684aa57faddedd2263f74279a637dbb9be1cff35378026.jpg)

Real GDP grew 1.0%ar in 4Q23, below our more optimistic forecast of 1.5%ar. The growth breakdown generally aligned with our expectations, however. Growth was led by consump tion and net exports but partially dragged by weak business fixed investment, including inventories, and government spending. 3Q23 growth was revised favorably to -0.5%,ar from -1.1%. Real consumer spending has sagged significantly since the second quarter of last year despite a positive mix of buoyant wage growth and a decent hangover of pandemic savings. The shift appears to be a foot vote linked to the historic hit to consumers<sup>’</sup> own personal finances. Households are facing a cascading increase in debt-servicing costs making them more cautious in their spending, motivated by the uncertainty of looming higher monthly payments. The consumption data bolster a sense of commitment to caution that consumers have affirmed in surveys (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Household real consumption %pt contribution to real GDP growth, saar
![](images/b77411710bd38ad0d1f8ee84457c3cbb159933bcd496b5fbeecd00f2722acca9.jpg)

Real GDP by industry rose 0.6%ar in 4Q23, after edging down in the previous quarter. Goods-producing industries increased 1.0%ar, following two consecutive quarterly declines. Services-producing industries rose 0.5%, growing for the 10th consecutive quarter albeit at a decelerating pace. Extraction was the largest driver of real GDP growth last quarter with oil and gas extraction dominating the lift. The manufacturing sector was down 1.3%ar in the fourth quarter, with transportation equipment manufacturing contributing the most to the decline. Weakness in the motor vehicle and parts manufacturing industry dominated, as several assembly plants shutdown for retooling. The manufacturing PMI is pointing to some positive momentum at the start of the year.

Figure 3: Real manufacturing output and Manufacturing PMI
![](images/794496636648b8769e391aa74e872a7dc81ad5560b579319ad022c7d94ad1353.jpg)

## Mexico

Mixed views in the 4Q23 inflation report but majority of the board seems ready to discuss a cut in March

Feb CPI up a modest 0.12%m/m (4.4%oya); the corrections in raw food and core goods continue

• Net exports remain a drag but investment and consumption still spearheading growth

Green light to electoral race with main focus on legislative fight

The Quarterly Inflation Report (QIR) from the central bank is a backward-looking release that emphasizes the assessment of economic conditions in the previous quarter. The report used to focus on the governor<sup>’</sup>s remarks with little participation from the deputy governors. However, the last few releases gradually have shown more active participation by board members during the Q&A session. This week, the QIR from 4Q23 moved a step forward given a more open discussion that allowed us to better understand the bias of the board.

The governor highlighted the disinflation gains in the context of an ongoing correction in expectations. Still, her emphasis on some conflicting signals, with goods on a much smoother path than services, was broadly in line with the rest of the board, suggesting that going forward the board will concentrate on crafting a cautious message that underscores the need to ease gradually.

Thus with the increased participation from the board during the Q&A section, it was evident, in our view, that most members are willing to start discussing a cut as soon as in March. The board further underlined the“passive tightening<sup>”</sup> in place since the end of the hiking cycle in March 2023 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Ex-ante real policy rate
![](images/90b953222e868c77e2bfd9a77276b8774f82832874062d0e7c5564654610ba2a.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Ex-ante policy rate range is Banxico's. Outlook updated Mar 1, 2024

With inflation expectations now converging to the long-term 3.5% level and the policy rate at 11.25%, the real ex-ante rate has increased nearly 110bp, bringing the ex-ante policy rate to 7.4% in February, a multi-year high.

To this point, another member emphasized the current misalignment between the ex-ante real rate and the neutral rate (above two times), suggesting it is time to level up these gauges. In previous episodes when inflation was above 4%, the real-rate gap has been below one. This additional evidence on the degree of tightness suggests four members would vote in favor of a cut soon. Still two members continue to call for caution, underscoring that the board should not precommit to an easing cycle, but assess the evolution of inflation on a case-by-case basis. Finally, both the downward revision to growth for 2024 (from 3% to 2.8%) and the Fed narrative in recent months were on the sidelines of the discussion.

## February CPI to deliver the goods

Next week<sup>’</sup>s final CPI release for February will likely deliver mixed news, but enough to support a rate cut from Banxico on March 21. Inflation is eyed at a modest 0.12%m/m, a print that builds on further declines in non-core prices, particularly fruits and vegetables. We expect non-core to skim 24bp from headline inflation even if some energy components and meat reverse some of the gains observed in 1H Feb. We see core inflation increasing 0.49%m/m, but in seasonally adjusted terms, it is further improving, and now at a run rate of 4.36%3m/3m, saar.

Within core, we continue to see some divergences. Core goods should continue to behave, continuing to deliver a stronger performance than its 10-year average and expected to decline further to 3.6%3m/3m saar (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Core components in Feb 2024 vs Feb 10-year pre-2020 avg %m/m
![](images/05baccca10dffe44e12f5d13b16ca10cebb3f3d20d920310395c9f43c9f6a82b.jpg)

Services, on the other hand, remain Banxico<sup>’</sup>s main source of concern: While education and housing continue to gradually improve—expected at 0.42%samr and 0.37%, respectively— “Other services<sup>”</sup> remains sticky, tracking 6.8%3m/3m saar in February. Core services as a whole is eyed at 5.3% and expected to stall around that level in 1H24. All in, annual inflation is eyed at 4.4% from 4.9%, with core also moderating, from 4.8% to 4.6%. Both are consistent with our yearend forecasts of 3.9% for both gauges.

## Mixed demand-side messages

While January trade data were somewhat negative on the back of disappointing external demand, they remain broadly supportive of our growth narrative for 1Q24: we expect activity to remain anchored on domestic demand. Looking at the details, manufacturing exports were down about 3%m/m on the back of a near-9% drop in auto-related exports, which have remained in a see-saw pattern since mid-2023 (Figure 3). We had pointed out that manufacturing indicators in January had thus far looked ok, with PMIs and business surveys pointing in the right direction.

Figure 3: Intermediate imports and auto exports as share of mfg exports % of total Inverted values
![](images/f00dbad353eb5a782e3e8e28eacbcc0c94e05c74b28712f886b6fcef9707f091.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from INEG

The import data were—intermediate imports aside—reasonably strong. Non-oil consumer goods rose again and the 3m/3m annualized run rate stood at about 20%, sa through January. We continue to anchor our more constructive 1H24 story on both strong consumption and investment even if both took a breather at the end of last year. Next week, we expect December personal consumption expenditure to decline 0.2%samr, while GFI is also expected at a weak -0.7%.

Coming back to the TB report, capital imports posted their second monthly gain after stalling in October/November, rising more than 3% in January, an encouraging message for our more constructive view for 1H24. We believe fixed investment will also resume its pre-4Q23 firm performance, even if the double-digit pace is clearly losing traction.

## Green light to the electoral process

This weekend, the electoral race for the June 2 federal election officially starts. While the 20%-pt advantage in favor of the incumbent Morena party (59% vs. 36% according to a recent poll from Buendia & Márquez) in the presidential race seems insurmountable, the focus has turned now to the legislative process and the nine state elections.

Regarding the former, it remains unclear if Morena will also approach the qualified (two-thirds) majority in both chambers, as a lot will depend on a potential coalition with Movimiento Ciudadano, the social democracy party that lost significant support after its presidential candidate was obliged to resign and now has approached Morena in an attempt to regain political leverage.

On the latter, Morena leads electoral preferences in seven out of nine states in which gubernatorial elections will take place, which would increase the incumbent party<sup>’</sup>s strength significantly, potentially controlling nearly 75% of the states.

## Brazil

• GDP stalled in the second half of last year, as expected

• We look for a re-acceleration this year, with GDP rising 2.2%y/y in 2024

• Labor market remained tight at the beginning of 1Q

• February inflation preview was high, but not as high as we anticipated

Our view has been that, following a weak 2H23, Brazil is well positioned to continue growing this year. The data so far, including this week<sup>’</sup>s 4Q GDP, look consistent with that view. Following a growth rate close to zero in 3Q, GDP decreased 0.1%q/q, saar in 4Q23, roughly in line with our call for stability, and slightly below the consensus of 0.4%q/q, saar. But the underlying trend of GDP looks healthy: outside of the agricultural sector, which grew more than 110%q/q, saar in 1Q23 and then has been negatively contributing to quarterly GDP growth, the economy expanded at a pace around 2%q/q, saar in the last two quarters of 2023 (Figure 1). We adjusted our 2024 GDP forecast from 1.6% to 2.2%y/y on revising our seasonal adjustment, with upward changes for over-year ago numbers.

Figure 1: GDP growth and GDP ex-agriculture
![](images/855b966edd72ab443bc4b894b5fb8244f4e8be60b679e0acdf446674502b9e0c.jpg)

As the agricultural shock fades, we expect the underlying GDP trend to be more visible at the start of this year. We look for a re-acceleration of GDP growth above 2%q/q, saar in the first half of this year as the strength of aggregate balance sheet of households, companies, and banks—partly at the expense of a weaker government balance sheet—should provide space for a solid rebound (Figure 2).

The recent data seem to support that call: the PMIs recovered at the beginning of the year, with this week<sup>’</sup>s February manufacturing PMI rising to the highest level since mid-2022, and the labor market remained tight with significant real wage gains in January. In fact, putting all numbers available together, our nowcaster is running above our call for 1Q GDP growth but it is still not considering the January hard data, which should be weak.

Figure 2: Quarterly real GDP growth
![](images/c3b4aa76edaad03190d5136b34e64352e0d9a8b28885d85ac514778e18bc54aa.jpg)

## Labor market remains tight

The January report continues signaling strong labor market conditions. The unemployment rate moved to 7.6% (nsa), a tenth below our call and two-tenths below the consensus. Seasonally adjusted, the jobless rate came down further to 7.8%, with our monthly proxy at the cycle<sup>’</sup>s lows.

The improvement comes from stronger employment growth, which expanded 0.3%m/m, sa for the second month in a row, and despite another month of recovery of the participation rate—that nonetheless is still historically low. The solid employment growth has been widespread among formal and informal jobs, and across different sectors.

Average real wages gained to 0.5%m/m, sa, with usually received wages accelerating from 3.1% to 3.8%oya and nominal gains from 8% to 8.6%. Other measures of nominal wages also went up in January. Our proxy of real disposable income growth increased a tenth to 8.7% in January (Figure 3), as the stronger labor income is compensated by a slowdown in income from transfers, though still indicating that consumption growth is likely to remain solid.

![](images/7c88c8fd3d2f78a904f5f57401c6b88bc42b2e9af3fe1b27f426520fad526c08.jpg)

## CPI preview was not as high as anticipated

Our case for 2024 CPI is for a below-consensus and benign year-end at 3.5%, but with a setback in 1Q. February<sup>’</sup>s CPI preview (IPCA-15) was not as high as we forecasted, rising 0.78%m/m, nsa, which was less than the consensus of 0.83% and our call at 0.90%, but the composition is aligned with the deterioration of underlying inflation this quarter (Figure 4). The surprise in core was more contained, rising 0.56% vs our expectations for a 0.60% increase, with the bulk of the surprise coming from in core services, 8bp below our call, whereas core goods prices rose more than initially predicted.

Even so, the lower core CPI and core services were not enough to change the recent upward behavior of underlying inflation. Seasonally adjusted core IPCA-15 rose just under 5%m/m, saar, and the 3-month average accelerated to 4%3m/3m, saar, the highest level since June 2023. Meanwhile, core services accelerated to above 6%m/m, saar, with the 3-month average topping the ceiling of the target range for the second month in a row. The deflation coming out of core goods also faded and vehicle prices in particular rose substantially in this IPCA-15.

The lower-than-expected but still worrisome CPI trend in the first part of February doesn<sup>’</sup>t modify our views. We see the recent worsening of core CPI as temporary and thus followed by lower numbers particularly in 2Q.

![](images/6b3d91b1991b06a195efcf028ea111fafef725ae5b9b53ecd829539241d27954.jpg)

## Argentina

Following the initial policy changes, the BCRA has bought US\$8.3bn in reserves since December

• The administration<sup>’</sup>s import payments framework has had a strong impact on reserves accumulation

• The FX gap compression has been mainly explained by exporters’ supply of financial FX

• Our base-case scenario is for net reserves accumulation at US\$10bn this year

• Economic activity is to continue suffering in 1H24

Following the initial policy changes, the BCRA has bought US\$8.3bn in reserves since December 10 (consistent with a daily average of US\$164mn). In our estimates, net FX reserves stand now at -US\$7.4bn (of note, our estimate includes Bopreal series 2 as a hard-currency liability), and USD liquid reserves at around US\$8.5bn. Of note, the latter compares with almost zero when the administration took office. Regarding the FX gap, YTD it has narrowed substantially despite intra-month volatility, now standing at around 30% (Figure 1). This is against the backdrop of the bulk of the financial controls being maintained for now, and a strong commitment to fiscal adjustment.

Figure 1: Reserves accumulation and FX gap US\$mn lhs, % rhs
![](images/737d14d4cd6d30796888c58ab169932724b23455b05ea485b359ffc295337e04.jpg)

The administration<sup>’</sup>s import payments framework has had a strong impact on the ability to accumulate reserves. The new import system allows payments to start only at 25% of the total amount imported one month later. This has helped the BCRA to accumulate reserves, at the cost of accumulating commercial (imports) debt. Indeed, in the first two months of Milei<sup>’</sup>s administration, we estimate new commercial debt has increased by US\$6.6bn (Figure 2). That compares with the central bank buying US\$5.7bn in the official FX market in the same period. While the activity contraction is expected to continue adding downward pressure on imported quantities, once (and if) imports payments start to normalize the reserves accumulation should slow down (all else equal).

Figure 2: Non-energy imports commercial debt US\$bn
![](images/a49283483183f340be456ec0da48d782269ca6076a7ccddee86e2408e4d34a0b.jpg)
Source: BCRA and J.P. Morgan

Meanwhile, the BCRA has continued with its strategy to deal with old commercial debt stock. Hard-currency paper from the central bank (Bopreal), underwritable in pesos, is being offered to those importers that accumulated commercial debt through December 11. So far, the BCRA has issued US\$7bn of an expected total amount of US\$10bn (an amount equivalent to the new commercial debt accumulation, as noted above). In other words, the “old<sup>”</sup> commercial debt excess stock will be unwound against the accumulation of hard-currency BCRA debt, a portion of which is expected to be cancelled (at the expense of lower future tax collection) if local companies buy the bond in the secondary market and use it to pay taxes.

Regarding the FX gap, the compression has been mainly explained by exporters<sup>’</sup> supply of financial FX (20% of total exports sales), and the bulk of capital controls remaining in place. The central bank estimates that there has been a supply of around US\$1.6bn in the parallel FX market coming from exports sales in between December and January (a US\$38mn daily average).

## External flows ahead

We expect the normalization of agricultural conditions to be supportive of a current account surplus this year. The good news is that El Niño should put drought conditions in the rearview mirror, consistent with a recovery in export inflows. We project total production for the main three crops (soybeans, corn, and wheat) to stand at US\$35bn (at current FOB prices). Translating production into exports, agri-exports would log US\$31bn, consistent with total exports at US\$79bn. That would imply a US\$13bn increase from 2023. On the other side, accrued imports are likely to plunge to US\$55bn following the FX discrete adjustment and economic activity downturn.

In terms of FX flows, we expect exports inflows to the official FX market of around US\$72bn this year, as 20% of total exports sales are supplied to the parallel FX market. Our base case assumes that the “Blend FX<sup>”</sup> will last until June, after which FX market unification should follow suit. Note a simple static exercise shows that should the “Blend FX<sup>”</sup> remain throughout the whole year, total export inflows to the official FX would stand at only US\$63bn (though dynamic exercise should also consider FX gap levels). Regarding FX outflows, we expect import payments at US\$47bn this year, a US\$8bn gap versus our accrued imports forecast. Private sector FX demand is expected to decrease by more than US\$5bn in 2024, mainly driven by less in tourism outflows and firms demand amid the recent FX discrete adjustment and activity contraction. The two main risks ahead are: i) That a widening of the FX gap (or the real undervaluation of the official FX) prompts agents to over-invoice imports and hoard crops again; and ii) that lower commodities prices or lack of rains drag on exports inflows.

Absent new external financing, the financial account is likely to continue proving a headwind on reserves accumulation. First, there is hard currency debt service of around US\$4.9bn remaining for this year, as US\$1.6bn in interest to bondholders and US\$2.8bn in amortizations to IFIs were already paid in January. On the Treasury side, this includes US\$2.7bn with bondholders to be paid on July 9 and net payments to the IMF and other IFIs of US\$1.2bn, given the disbursements of the revised IMF program (see note). Moreover, the BCRA has to meet monthly capital amortizations of series 2 Bopreal since July totaling US\$1bn. We also assume that the US\$5bn tapped from the PBoC swap is fully rolled-over. Additional financial outflow pressure could come from both the remission abroad of retained earnings, and lower reinvestment of earnings if the government eventually releases capital controls.

Against this backdrop, our base case is for net reserves accumulation to be US\$10bn this year, in line with the IMF<sup>’</sup>s target. While the net reserves gain would be equivalent to 2% of GDP, the level would still prove low in absolute terms. While this could be associated with a challenge ahead in terms of the removal of capital controls and FX market unification, we believe that such a policy measure would only secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in 2025-26.

## Activity downturn

As expected, activity nosedived in December, evidence of the costs associated with the required correction of the macro imbalances. After three months of poor performance, activity lost 3.1%m/m sa, with both secondary and tertiary activity in the red. The 3-month sequential growth plunged by 7.3% ar, which compares with +11.3% in 3Q23. The plunge came even with the sequential recovery in agriculture production amid the normalization of weather and crop conditions. When excluding agricultural activity, the sequential activity pace moved deeper into negative territory, running at -10%ar.

The activity drop was concurrent with the inflation leap that followed the discrete FX correction and he beginning of the fiscal adjustment effort. The performance of some sectors such as manufacturing, commerce, and construction is reminiscent of a sudden-stop financial crisis. On an over-year-ago basis, and pending revisions, activity contracted by 4.5%oya, and 1.6%y/y.

As mentioned in previous notes, three factors are aligning to spark a severe adjustment in domestic demand, particularly in 1H24: the re-calibration of the official FX and its consequent inflationary acceleration, the real erosion of income and money aggregates amid the need to correct relative prices and very negative real rates, and the draconian fiscal adjustment to which the government is committed. Yet, the recovery of agricultural production, which subtracted close to 2%-pts from 2023 annual GDP, is likely to offset in part the headline activity loss.

We maintain our forecast for sequential growth to prove negative in the first half of the year, recovering by the second half on the assumption that the stabilization effort proves successful, with GDP growing at a +3.5%ar average pace. This path would be consistent with annual GDP at -3.0%y/y.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Chile: Activity starts the year on the right foot

• Unemployment inches lower, job creation gains momentum

• Peru: Sequential GDP growth firmed up in 4Q23

El Niño’s feared impact on activity muted so far, skewing our+2.1%y/y growth projection higher

## Chile: Activity starts 2024 on the right foot

Headline activity gained 1.7%m/m, sa, in January, on the heels of the 1.0 % pothole recorded in December (revised from -1.1%, previously), with both mining and ex. mining activity recording positive variations, adding 0.8%-pt and 0.9%-pt on the month to headline activity, respectively (Figure 1). When the last three-month sequential performance is analyzed, upon revisions, headline activity is running at +1.9 %3m/3m, saar, up from 4Q23<sup>’</sup>s 0.8 % (revised higher, from 0.4% previously).

Figure 1: Chile economic activity
![](images/33b886e94ac823b9f437a79769b229d0e7d92bbee468520ff0100dff1ae3808b.jpg)

As usual, we focus on ex. mining activity, the most relevant indicator from a cyclical standpoint. Ex.mining recorded +1.1 %m/m, sa, after a 0.5% drop in December. A more granular analysis shows services, industry, and commerce up on the month, by +1.0%, 1.1%, and 1.2%, respectively. Finally, always choppy mining production bounced back in January, up by 6.1 %sa on the month.

January<sup>’</sup>s activity data align with our base case for firmer growth ahead, as the drag from restrictive monetary policy ebbs. Our base case is for ex. mining growth to converge to its potential in 2024. We thus foresee a very shallow negative output gap ahead (defined for ex. mining activity), which would be consistent with the monetary policy rate converging to the level we deem neutral over the year. Granted, the speed of such convergence depends on how the narrowing of the interest rate differential affects market conditions, with the board<sup>’</sup>s decision process preventing excess volatility from impairing monetary policy transmission channels.

In terms of forecast, while waiting for the 4Q23 national accounts report to be published mid-March, the projection for the 1Q24 is revised higher to +3.9%q/q, saar, from 2.6% previously. The projection for the year is also maintained at +2.0%y/y (consistent with +2.8%Q4/Q4), though signaling upside risk.

## Peru: Sequential growth firmed up in 4Q23

Peru<sup>’</sup>s national accounts reported this week confirmed the recovery of GDP growth, though it remains below potential. Sequentially, real GDP gained 2.1%ar, on the heels of a revised +0.6%ar observed for 3Q23. Thus, growth seems to be gaining momentum, after the disappointing 2Q and 3Q, when activity failed to regain pre-social unrest levels. Indeed, the stagnant recovery pace after the social unrest and adverse weather event in the first quarter of the year pushed annual GDP into the red, at -0.6%y/y (Figure 2).

## Figure 2: Peru real GDP

4Q19=100, dotted curve corresponds to 2014-19 trend growth implied levels
![](images/132a6ba8461840192d84a87e6c10528a1b499b45514cb903e0c0688240dbb09a.jpg)

When analyzing the year as a whole, domestic demand posted negative annual growth, down 1.7%y/y, basically on a contraction in investment. Households eked out a gain, up by 0.1%y/y, well below the 3.3% average growth observed in 2014-19. On the other side, public sector consumption posted +3.0%y/y, above the 2.7% average. The poor household consumption performance over the year echoed the 7.3%y/y plunge recorded in private sector fixed investment. Finally, the ray of light proved to be real exports, which contributed positively due to real exported volume gains and also to an import contraction in line with domestic demand behavior. Yet,exports growth, up by+3.8%y/y, fell short of the +5.3% average observed in 2014-19.

Ahead, our modal scenario is for activity to regain momentum. Less of a monetary restriction should dominate over a very moderate fiscal headwind, with private sector consumption recovering and investment stabilizing. Importantly, the feared impact of El Niño on activity has so far proved relatively muted, which skews our annual +2.1%y/y growth projection higher.

## United Kingdom

• Lombardelli announced as the next BoE Deputy Governor for monetary policy

Ramsden floats the idea of selling all of the QE gilt portfolio

• Expecting a small tax cut from next week<sup>’</sup>s Budget, even with limited headroom

• Mortgage approvals rise in January, as housing revival continues

Clare Lombardelli was announced as the replacement for Ben Broadbent as the BoE<sup>’</sup>s Deputy Governor for Monetary Policy this week, effective from July. She is currently Chief Economist at the OECD and was previously the Chief Economic Advisor to the Treasury. It<sup>’</sup>s hard to know how this will affect the balance on the MPC. Internal members are much less likely to dissent than externals, and so the odds on this affecting vote outturns this year look more limited. Lombarelli<sup>’</sup>s presence is perhaps more likely to show up in her ability to affect the debate and influence thinking on the committee.

Dave Ramsden gave a speech this week in which he made the point that the BoE could, if it wished to, sell all of its remaining gilt portfolio and use liquidity operations to ensure that doesn<sup>’</sup>t create unwanted effects in the interbank market. There there was no new information in Ramsden<sup>’</sup>s speech about how the plumbing of this operation can work. But his decision to highlight the possibility that the BoE could unwind the full gilt portfolio may be an early hint that the Bank is thinking about moving down this path. There are reasons why the BoE may prefer this option, as it would enable it to demonstrate to the doubters that it is prepared to use QE policy symmetrically - something that has received political scrutiny on from the House of Lords. The decision on whether on not to fully sell all of the gilts - as opposed to stopping when the level of reserves reaches its equilibrium - will probably not have to be made until 2027. Prior to then the BoE is likely to continue shrinking its balance sheet through gilt unwind, as the level of reserves will still be higher than banks require.

## Budget preview

A few week<sup>’</sup>s ago we estimated there would be an extra £7bn of fiscal headroom for the Chancellor to use at next week<sup>’</sup>s Budget. This would be sufficient to enable a 1%-pt cut in the basic rate of income tax, while leaving headroom unchanged from the OBR<sup>’</sup>s £13bn estimate from last November. In recent weeks, however, there have been briefings to the media suggesting that the increase in headroom is not as large as our estimate.

Based on the window used by the OBR for its market assumptions, interest rates are still lower than assumed back at last year<sup>’</sup>s Autumn Statement, There should hence be some more headroom available through this source. It is possible there are some other adjustments or mitigating factors the OBR has taken into account that we have not, but it would be a surprise to us if the pre-measures headroom has not increased at all from £13bn. We hence stick with our original estimate from last month.

Source: J.P. Morgan expectations

The Chancellor has commented that there isn<sup>’</sup>t as much room to cut taxes as last Autumn. However, the literal interpretation of these remarks doesn<sup>’</sup>t rule out tax cuts given there was around £14bn of easing announced last November (for the 2025-26 period). Easing by less than this figure would still be consistent with the £7bn figure we mention above.

If for some reason there is less room available than we estimate, the Chancellor would still have the following options if he is minded to cut income tax: 1) Choose a cheaper tax cut such as employee national insurance, worth closer to £5bn 2) Eat further into the existing headroom than before 3) Implicitly pledge to cut spending by more after this year<sup>’</sup>s election 4) Raise revenue from another source. All options have been discussed in the media recently, but our best guess it that it won<sup>’</sup>t come to that unless the Chancellor opts for a larger tax cut than we describe above.

We think the broader economic and policy implications of a small tax cut will be limited. While it will support growth from this Spring, the bigger issue is whether the consumer starts spending the gains from their improving real incomes in 1H24. In a further sign of a return to life in the housing market, mortgage approvals jumped by 3.7k to 55.2 in January (Figure 1), the highest since October 2022 (just after the mini-Budget). Nationwide also reported another 0.7%m/m rise in house prices for February. This is another hint that something broader may be at play, i.e. growth may be about to start lifting. And if the Chancellor attempts to focus tax cuts on lifting labor participation, the OBR and BoE are likely to again claim that the inflation implications are more limited. We would dispute the idea that labour supply will be materially lifted by lower taxes. But even so, the easing is probably small enough not to have a large inflation impact.
