## Emerging Europe

Israel: The BoI on hold, signaling cautious approach to cuts

• Hungary: NBH speeds up the pace of easing (temporarily)

• Türkiye: Strong 4Q23 GDP growth despite rate hikes

• Russia: Growth started 2024 on solid note

## Israel: Proceed with caution

The BoI left the policy rate unchanged at 4.5% at this week<sup>’</sup>s meeting, surprising us and consensus expectations (-25bp). Soft inflation and growth data were not enough for the MPC to proceed with back-to-back cuts. The tone of the press release did not change much from the previous meeting. That said, the statement and Governor Yaron noted easing constraints on demand amid continued constraints on supply, particularly in construction. The Governor also focused on still high uncertainty with respect to the intensity and duration of the war, as well as its impact on the inflation process. Expansionary fiscal policy and the recent rise in inflation expectations added to inflationary risks.

Despite the hawkish surprise, the Governor made it clear that the BoI preserves the easing bias and the mid-term guidance remains unchanged – rates will move lower once the BoI gains more confidence about inflation stabilizing at low levels. That said, the MPC wants to avoid the mistake of cutting rates too rapidly. A less front-loaded policy easing in DM was mentioned in communications, but not stressed as major driver of BoI decision.

We continue to see room for further moderate monetary policy easing in Israel and don<sup>’</sup>t see obvious macro or market obstacles for it in the near term. That said, we take on board the BoI<sup>’</sup>s desire to tread carefully and now expect a less frontloaded policy path. We now expect the BoI to cut at every other meeting, but continue to expect the terminal rate at 3.75% by year-end. We continue to see loose fiscal policy as the main constraint on monetary policy easing in mid-term.

## Hungary: NBH speeds pace of easing

After holding in January, the NBH cut the policy rate by 100bps to 9% (Figure 1), accelerating the easing pace from the previous 75bp/meeting. The statement justified this acceleration, which it characterizes as “temporary<sup>”</sup>, with strongerthan-expected disinflation in recent months, a low demand environment and improved risk perceptions.

![](images/91a87382a2d617171bcf623cf0cc6c893cf21e7accd52b70120f519a5a0fd782.jpg)

Although the pace of cuts picked up, the press conference confirmed that this is a temporary fine-tuning of the easing cycle, with the usual prudent guidance practically unchanged. According to Deputy Governor Virag, a disciplined and “stability-oriented<sup>”</sup> monetary policy is still required, market expectations for rates of 6-7% around mid-year “appear realistic<sup>”</sup>, and the NBH will aim for an “appropriately positive<sup>”</sup> real rate environment.

Given the collapse in Hungarian inflation, the positive current account balance and crucially, the positive evolution of political risk events (EU fund deal, approval of Ukraine aid, agreement to Sweden<sup>’</sup>s NATO accession) its hard to justify why Hungary should run near-10% interest rates. Its regional peers have not-too-dissimilar inflation rates and practically half the interest rate. Cutting rates marginally faster, starting from a higher level than anyone else, makes sense in our view, especially as the guidance suggests sensibly that such pace is to be maintained only temporarily.

We expect the 100bp/meeting pace will last for just two more meetings, February and March, when we see the policy rate falling to 8%. After that, we see the pace of cuts being gradually reduced, with the policy rate at 6-7% mid-year and reaching 5.25% at the end of the year. Recently the Forint has been under some weakening pressure, and for now the NBH decided to look through that weakness (correctly in our opinion). But we know how FX-sensitive the NBH can be. If that trend were to worsen, we could imagine the rate path shifting somewhat in the cautious direction. But given Hungary<sup>’</sup>s macro fundamentals and the NBH<sup>’</sup>s prudent stance, we struggle to see why the currency should weaken materially in the first place.

Figure 2: NBH interest rate outlook
![](images/fac1a64bb998d6223a48b9c865e5fa0a5598b40e08c24ff8f21acc96ca92bd6b.jpg)

## Türkiye: Strong 4Q23 GDP growth

GDP growth accelerated to 1.0% q/q sa in 4Q23, versus 0.3% in 3Q23. It was mainly driven by household consumption, which rebounded by 3.6% q/q sa in 4Q23 versus -1.7% in 3Q23. Investments and government consumption made negative contributions to the sequential growth in 4Q23. Real exports fell by 2.5% q/q sa, while imports declined by more 3.9% q/q sa as stronger domestic demand was driven by the services sector in 4Q23. Overall, we think that the sequential GDP growth composition was not helpful for disinflation as household spending rebounded in 4Q23.

In over year ago terms, GDP growth was strong at 4.0% oya in 4Q23. The contribution to over-year-ago GDP growth from household consumption declined, while that from net exports improved in 4Q23, partly due to favorable base effects. Household consumption contributed 6.7pp in 4Q23, down from 7.7pp in 3Q23. The negative contribution of net exports to annual growth improved from 2.6pp in 3Q23 to 0.5pp in 4Q23 (Figure 3). Türkiye<sup>’</sup>s GDP growth ended 2023 at 4.5% oya, down from 5.5% in 2022. Despite the slight slowdown in yearly growth, we estimate an output gap of +1.5% in 2023. The level of economic activity continued to exert upward pressure on inflation and current account deficit in 2023.

Figure 3. Real GDP by expenditure contributions
![](images/e4fd05e3578c6637309d8a55e494358412f745e942031516bf5f4f0bd7598ff8.jpg)
\* Household consumption + government consumption \*\* Gross Fixed capital formation + inventory changes \*\*\* Exports - imports

Economic activity is likely to be stronger in 1Q24 due to wage hikes and fiscal stimulus. Leading indicators signal strong sequential quarterly growth in 1Q24 as household consumption picked up. Credit card expenditures and consumer loan growth accelerated in Jan-Feb, which is a good leading indicator for household consumption. The consumer confidence index jumped to 79.88 in Jan-Feb, from an average of 75.84 in 4Q23, thanks to wage hikes. The manufacturing PMI rebounded to 49.2 in January versus an average of 47.7 in 4Q23.

Given these factors, we now see upside risks to our GDP growth forecast of 2.7% this year. In our view, the current monetary-fiscal policy mix is not tight enough to cool household consumption growth. It will be important to see how monetary and fiscal policies shift in the aftermath of March municipal elections to re-assess our GDP growth and inflation forecasts.

## Russia: Growth remains solid

January activity data suggest that growth inertia remains substantial and the economy continues expanding, albeit at slower pace. Industrial production edged up 0.2%m/m, sa, in January, with mining up 0.3% and manufacturing perking 0.2%. Within manufacturing, industries related to arms procurement fared well last month, while the rest of the complex continue to stagnate.

The bigger picture is that manufacturing has stagnated for nine months by now, but continued consumer and investment demand growth suggest that manufacturing may pick up pace in coming month — the manufacturing PMI jumping to 54.7 in February, its multi-year high, supports this view. Construction dropped 3.2%m/m, sa, in January, following a similarly large increase the prior month. Transport turnover rose 1.4%, while wholesale trade turnover was flat on the month. We estimate that the index of basic sectors — a good GDP proxy remained unchanged in January, following a strong 2.3% increase in previous month. We lifted our tracking of 1Q growth marginally higher (0.75 saar), but may need to revise it once a revised quarterly GDP trajectory is published by Rosstat in coming weeks. Risks to our forecast are skewed to upside.

Retail sales rose 1.1%m/m, sa, in January, following two months of stagnation, with solid gains in both food and nonfood sales. Our inclination is to treat this gain with caution, as January sales data tend to be volatile due to the long holiday season. Tighter monetary conditions should continue to pull the saving rate higher in the coming months, weighing on consumption. However, still strong income growth should allow for modest consumption gains, in our view. Uninterrupted growth in consumption of services — up 0.5% last month — is a sign that the underlying momentum in consumer demand remains solid.

The labor market remained tight through January. Both the labor force (-0.3) and employment (-0.1) registered monthly declines, which, we suspect, could be at least partially due to the annual change in the sample for the household survey. The unemployment rate slid further to 2.8%, sa, another record low. Nominal wage growth accelerated to 16.6%oya in December, with private sector wages continuing growing well above productivity, which should continue exerting upward pressure on services inflation in coming months.

## Poland:

<table><tr><td>Wed</td><td>NBP rate decision</td></tr><tr><td>Mar 6</td><td>unchanged at 5.75%</td></tr></table>

We expect the NBP to keep rates unchanged at 5.75%. Inflation is falling but remains elevated, and the central bank is concerned about the impact of potential regulatory changes impacting the prices of food and energy. Governor Glapinski suggested at the last press conference that rates could be on hold all year, displaying absolutely no dovish bias to cut rates near term.

We expect headline CPI to rise 4.2% m/m in February mainly due to the impact of Ramadan on food and restaurant prices and the effects of Brent oil prices on fuel prices. Headline over-year-ago inflation is set to increase from 64.9% oya in January to 66.5% in February. We forecast headline inflation to peak at 73.4% in May 2024, and end 2024 at 42.2% with risks on the upside due to sticky services prices.

Source: TUIK, CBRT, J.P. Morgan forecast

## South Africa

• January fiscal balance beats market expectations based on issuance

• Yet our deficit tracking marginally widens to 5% (from 4.9%; Treasury: 4.7%)

• 4Q23 GDP probably gained 0.4%q/q (1.4%q/q ar)

• 4Q23 CAD likely wider at -1.8% of GDP (from -0.3%)

National Treasury recently presented a conservative Budget for FY24/25, despite economic pressures and nearing national elections. The focus remained on spending restraint and incremental tax measures to enable a near-term stabilization in the debt ratio. This led Treasury to project an improving fiscal balance with a 4.3% (JPMe: 4.9%) deficit in FY24/25 and 3.9% in FY25/26, from 4.7% in FY23/24 (JPMe: 4.9%). While the Budget overall marginally improved our fiscal outlook, predominantly due to the front-loaded disbursement from the GFECRA, a key issue was that expenditure tracked well ahead of plan in FY23/24 with little clarity in the Budget. The fiscal year ends in March.

Revenue is now broadly on track to reach its target. The revenue to GDP ratio remained steady at 24.4% in January, although this is well below its peak of 25.4% in March (Figure 1). The rolling fiscal deficit improved to 5.5% of GDP (from 6%) as the fiscal shortfall this January was substantially narrower than last year, when SOEs received a payment of 0.3%pts of GDP during the month. The cumulative fiscal deficit for the first ten months of FY23/24 has reached 5% of GDP by January, from 4.2% in December. Typically, seasonality suggests that fiscal balances are jointly marginally negative in February and March, making the execution of the 4.7% official deficit forecast less likely.

Figure 1: Revenue ratio and rolling fiscal deficit
![](images/9b14b0b7a4f04d1511267379fc9cf0956c0ae9bf2538a77f88a732c6bd2fb3bc.jpg)

The January data suggest that expenditure tracking ahead of plan has slowed somewhat, but the gap remains significant. Expenditure contracted 1%oya in January when adjusting for the inflated baseline from disbursements to SOE<sup>’</sup>s last year, from 4.6% in 4Q23. This leaves cumulative spending for the fiscal year to date now at 83% of target, against a historical benchmark of 80.8%. We think the gap to expected spending now peaked in December at 2.7% of target (0.9%pts of GDP) and reduced to 2.2% of target (0.7%pts) in January. Even so, we continue to think that this will risk a combination of accruals (with payment in the first few months of the next fiscal year), acute spending restraint and possibly a further special appropriation before end-March. We now project a 5% fiscal deficit for the current year, with risks of greater spending pressures in April, from 4.9% previously (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Fiscal deficit based (cumulative)
![](images/1232742a38c6f287a673b342691dceb4bcf1254d206e1ce16444622c2d2cc03a.jpg)

## 4Q23: GDP likely up 0.4%q/q, CAD widened

We expect the 4Q23 GDP report to show an uneven IP recovery underpinning a 0.4%q/q ( 1.4%q/q ar) GDP gain (Figure 3). Sequential tracking suggests mining output more than regained the 4.2%q/q ar it lost in 3Q with a 10.5% rise in 4Q. On the contrary, the recovery momentum in manufacturing output looks weak as we track a 0.6% increase for the quarter, following a 5% contraction in 3Q.

Figure 3: South Africa - Contributions to GDP
![](images/ccc532ee8806188ba81aacdddfe87db9951be09320e9e43efd422e01c9ae874e.jpg)
21.Q1 21.Q2 21.Q3 21.Q4 22.Q1 22.Q2 22.Q3 22.Q4 23.Q1 23.Q2 23.Q3 23.Q4 Source: StatsSA. J.P. Morgan calculations

The data suggest the manufacturing sector has been impacted most by the ongoing logistical challenges at the ports. In December output declined by 1.7%m/m (sa) as motor parts nose-dived by 28.4% (from +3.7%), and electrical machinery by 8.1% (+1.7%). These products are mostly shipped via rail and this mirrors the messaging from the external activity report for December where both these categories declined to shrink overall trade surplus. Performance in agriculture production tends to be volatility and hard to predict, which could drive surprises relative to our expectations.

Meanwhile, the weak consumption cycle appears to have extended amid tepid wage growth and a negative credit impulse. In particular, pressures from non-discretionary inflation has been high as reflected in vehicle and retail sales data disappointing in 4Q despite the December festive holidays. Our model tracking suggests a 1.7%q/q in 4Q fall in retail sales (from +3.8%), a 17.8% nosedive in wholesale trade, while vehicle sales remained in the red.

Separately, the 4Q23 external accounts will likely show a marked reduction in the trade surplus widening the current account deficit (CAD) to 1.8% of GDP in 4Q23, from 0.3% (Figure 4). We estimate full quarter trade balance at +1.1% of GDP, from +2.7% in 3Q. The trade balance surplus shrank to R5.9bn, sa in December, from R17.6bn as imports (+1.9%m/ m) edged up against a fall in exports (-4.8%). Chemical products led the rise in imports in November, while subdued terms of trade and logistical challenges continued to dent export growth. Our baseline assumes the shortfall on invisibles (Income, services and transfers) was stable at around 2.8%-3% of GDP, although details will only be published with the quarterly bulletin on March 28. If we are right, the full year CAD probably will average 1.4%of GDP for 2023.

Figure 4: South Africa current account balance
![](images/b679064a5abc38fafa8725586caed4aecd837504526f7b6deb3fa85f460b8ba4.jpg)
Source: SARB, J.P. Morgan calculations

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s monthly CPI indicator increased 3.4%oya in January, softer than expected

But many key services prices, which the RBA is focused on, are only updated later in the quarter

• We expect Australian 4Q23 real GDP to rise 0.4%q/q sa, an acceleration from 3Q’s pace

The RBNZ left the cash rate on hold at 5.5% in the February meeting

Australia<sup>’</sup>s monthly CPI indicator remained at 3.4%oya nsa in January, softer than J.P. Morgan (+3.7%oya) and consensus (+3.6%oya) expectations. The core indicators all came down, the ex-volatile items measure fell 0.1%-pt to 4.1%oya nsa and trimmed mean fell 0.2%-pt sa to 3.8%oya. This week<sup>’</sup>s number was another step in the right direction for the RBA, though the Bank is more focused on services inflation, and many key service prices will be updated later in the quarter.

The details showed recent dynamics broadly continued in January. Housing inflation remained strong in aggregate (+4.6%oya nsa, Figure 1), though growth in new dwelling prices was a touch softer than we expected. The impact of electricity price rises on the CPI continued to be blunted by subsidies. Annual electricity prices rose 0.8% nsa in January, although the ABS notes electricity prices would be up 15.3%oya nsa excluding the effects of subsidies.

Figure 1: Australian housing related inflation
![](images/0293c06eb51fd02eb53f587e7dcdc491f8339549526ad174d438d1d0a25796f3.jpg)

The RBA<sup>’</sup>s recent inflation commentary has mainly focused on stickiness within services inflation. In annual terms, goods prices declined in January (-0.1%-pt), while services rose modestly (+0.1%-pt). But, as flagged above, many services prices are imputed in the first month of the quarter, so it<sup>’</sup>s hard to judge the pace of services disinflation at this stage. We will receive further updates in February on items such as education, insurance and food services.

Still, much of the Bank<sup>’</sup>s vigilance on inflation has been predicated on keeping inflation expectations anchored at the target. Moderation in aggregate inflation should ease concerns about expectations unmooring, and weaker than expected goods inflation gives the Bank more leeway to allow services inflation, which tends to track headline inflation with a lag, to fall to target more gradually. Both this week<sup>’</sup>s inflation data and the January labor data were weaker than we expected. Although we think some of January<sup>’</sup>s labor market deterioration should be unwound, the data year to date increase the risks of the easing cycle commencing earlier than we currently anticipate(1Q25).

## Retail sales bounce in January

Australian retail sales were up 1.1%m/m in January, a bit below our and consensus expectations (+1.5%m/m), but the magnitude of December<sup>’</sup>s decline was revised down (prior: -2.7%m/m, revised -2.1%m/m). The retail data have been noisy of late, due to changing seasonality around the timing of discounting activity, so it<sup>’</sup>s been hard to be precise about the magnitude of month to month growth. We don<sup>’</sup>t read a lot into this week<sup>’</sup>s forecast miss, especially given the revised figures for December. In the details, non-food sectors bounced-back after seasonal weakness in December. The ABS notes food sales were bolstered by sporting events throughout the month; we think this could continue in February given activity within the music industry over the month.

Figure 2: Australia real GDP
![](images/7b9f863a8416cc7c32d604da743a532669d51151b3bd56d16f3b900beb92bb9a.jpg)

## Real GDP growth on the up in 4Q

Next week<sup>’</sup>s 4Q real GDP print will provide an important update on the underlying health of the Australian economy. We expect real GDP growth increased 0.4%q/q last quarter, a modest uptick from the prior quarter but still one of the softer quarterly outcomes of recent years. Retail volumes rose 0.3%q/q, an outcome close to our standing household consumption forecast and consistent with the view that spending remained lackluster late in the year. This week<sup>’</sup>s construction and private capex data were both weaker than we expected, imparting some modest downside risk to the GDP print. Early next week, the trade, inventories, profits and government spending data are set for release; we will revisit our GDP forecast following these releases.

New Zealand

## RBNZ defies hawkish expectations

The RBNZ left the cash rate unchanged at 5.5% at the February meeting, consistent with our forecast and the surveyed consensus. The Statement reiterated many of the themes from previous meetings, while the forward guidance was left broadly unchanged noting, “the OCR needs to remain at a restrictive level for a sustained period of time.<sup>”</sup>

We retain the view that the next move in the OCR will be lower, but with the RBNZ still expressing concern around the inflation outlook and only one further CPI print before the May meeting, we now forecast a first 25bp cut in August (previous forecast: May).

There were small tweaks to the OCR track, but in general the Bank<sup>’</sup>s projection remains consistent with the prior high hold scenario. Specifically, the 2024 peak in the projected OCR track was lowered slightly from 5.7% to 5.6%, while cuts are still signaled from early 2025.

Relative to November, this week<sup>’</sup>s minutes were less explicit regarding the discussion surrounding further rate hikes, though Governor Orr<sup>’</sup>s press conference made it clear the Committee still deems it too early to entertain policy easing, noting members, “certainly didn<sup>’</sup>t discuss a cut.<sup>”</sup>

## Greater China

• China: A preview of the NPC

• February PMIs point to steady near-term growth momentum

• Taiwan: Export orders rebounded, led by tech

IP beat expectations; 4Q GDP shows moderate demand growth trends

• Manufacturing PMI eased modestly

• Next week: China trade, FX reserves and inflation, Taiwan CPI and trade

China<sup>’</sup>s National People<sup>’</sup>s Congress (NPC) will begin on March 5, one day after the Chinese People<sup>’</sup>s Political Consultative Conference (together the two meetings are usually referred to as the “Two Sessions<sup>”</sup>). The NPC meeting is the annual event to approve the government<sup>’</sup>s work report, fiscal budget and other policy targets, and hence closely watched.

We expect the NPC will keep its annual growth target unchanged at “around 5%<sup>”</sup> in 2024, which will be a challenging task as the reopening dividend has largely faded. To achieve the target, we have argued that both fiscal and monetary policy will be more growth-friendly than 2023, but not a “bazooka<sup>”</sup> style stimulus. On fiscal policy, we look for a 3.8% of GDP budgetary deficit, which could be in the form of 3% official deficit plus 1 trillion yuan special central government bonds. In addition, we expect the quota of special local government bonds at 3.5 trillion yuan this year (vs. 3.8 trillion yuan in 2023). We expect the government will maintain its inflation target at 3% (a ceiling concept in practice), which is not binding as our forecast of average CPI this year is 0.6%. We expect the government will reiterate its monetary policy narrative with expanded use of structural monetary policy.

What could surprise us? If Premier Li Qiang<sup>’</sup>s first work report shares market concerns of economic challenges, including deflation risk, the structural unemployment issue and damage from the housing market correction, it will be a positive surprise for us. Another area of positive surprise could be the budgetary deficit. If the number surprises on the high side, this will suggest that the fiscal authority, especially the central government, will take more responsibility in growth stabilization efforts. If it is announced that the 1 trillion yuan special CGB scheme will become a multi-year program, or the NPC lifts the debt ceiling for local governments to create more room for local government hidden debt swap, it will be a positive surprise as well.

For the 3rd Plenum of the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress, it has been delayed and there is no clarity on a possible date. Our expectation for the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum is relatively low for two reasons.

First, there is no signal that the party will choose to deviate from the high-quality growth framework as laid out in the 19<sup>th</sup> Party Congress. Second, the market is looking for more action rather than policy reiteration, or looking for a directional change rather than continuation of current policy measures. From that perspective, the probability of upside surprise is low.

## PMIs point to steady growth momentum

China<sup>’</sup>s February manufacturing PMIs were in line with expectations, with a decent uptick in the NBS non-manufacturing PMIs suggesting steady near-term growth momentum. The NBS manufacturing PMI eased modestly by 0.1-pt to 49.1 (Figure 1), with the output component down 1.5-pt, largely reflecting the LNY seasonal factor. The Caixin manufacturing PMI, which tends to show less LNY seasonality, rose 0.1-pt to 50.9 in February (the highest reading in six months). The third consecutive monthly increase in the export orders component of the export-oriented Caixin manufacturing PMI is worth noting, hinting at somewhat constructive near-term outlook on export sector activity.

Figure 1: China manufacturing PMIs
![](images/33b0defd57ba0345aef34a530e8596a4563f123e33a0b66db12121cd917f3c9c.jpg)

The NBS non-manufacturing PMI shows solid gain of 0.7-pt to 51.4 in February (the highest reading in five months, Figure 2). The service activity component ticked up further by 0.9-pt to 51.0, largely reflecting solid pickup in travel-related activities during the Lunar New Year holiday. On the other hand, the construction activity index fell 0.4-pt to 53.4, reflecting impact of cold weather, as well as seasonal factor.

Putting together the manufacturing and non-manufacturing components, the NBS composite PMI output stayed unchanged at 50.9 (highest level since October), suggesting steady overall economic activity momentum. On the cautious side, housing market activity appears to remain sluggish. The employment component of the PMIs remains subdued, suggesting ongoing labor market sluggishness which will drag on household sentiment. Lingering softness in pricing power and general deflation concerns continue to stress corporate profit and cash flow, restraining the private investment outlook.

Figure 2: China NBS non-manufacturing PM Index, sa
![](images/f1f7e49eef9de569930877ca1e9ea70de1d1d9655f1dda67a67529ea4e1736f3.jpg)

## Hong Kong: Exports beat expectations

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s January merchandise exports came in above expectations on LNY seasonal effects, rising 33.6%oya to HK\$388.7bn, following an 11.0%oya rise in December. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that the city<sup>’</sup>s exports rose 2.0%m/m sa. Imports rose 21.7%oya on a 2.2%m/m sa fall. The monthly merchandise trade activities realized a surplus of HK\$3.6bn, the first time since January 2022.

Mainland China is the largest trade partner of HK SAR, accounting for 55.6% of the city<sup>’</sup>s exports and 43.5% of the imports in 2023. By our estimate, exports to mainland China rose 54.2%oya or 7.9%m/m sa in January while exports to Asia as a whole rose 45.7%oya. Exports to Japan fell 7.4%m/ m sa, and exports to the US fell 1.6%.

## The 2024-25 Budget

The Financial Secretary for Hong Kong delivered the FY24/25 budget speech on February 28, with newly announced economic supportive schemes. Current fiscal year FY23/24 is expected to record a deficit of HK\$101.6bn, larger than the original estimate of HK\$54.4bn. The government revenue was revised down by HK\$87.8bn to HK\$554.6bn, mainly due to the shortage from land premium and stamp duty. In particular, revenue from land premium is HK\$46.2bn lower than the original estimate, while revenue from stamp duty is HK\$35bn lower than the original estimate.

Given the trade-offs between supporting economic growth and restoring fiscal balance, Hong Kong will not rush to fiscal balance in 2024/25. The government announced to cancel all demand-side management measures for residential properties with immediate effect, as an effort to revive the property market. The Government anticipates FY24/25 total government expenditure to increase by 6.7%y/y to HK\$776.9bn, while revenue will increase to HK\$633.0bn. Considering the proceeds from issuance of government bonds of \~HK\$120bn, FY24/25 will post a fiscal deficit of HK\$48.1bn, or 1.5% of GDP based on our forecast (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Hong Kong fiscal balance
![](images/df168acc84432510bd133e95b68a38795fc4cca894a79b9b20eb5dfb07b74f35.jpg)
Source: HK Government, J.P. Morgan

## Retail sales fell 1.2% on LNY seasonality

Hong Kong<sup>’</sup>s retail sales volume came in significantly below expectations in January, falling 1.2%oya (Figure 4). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that retail sales volume fell 1.9%m/m sa in January, adding to the 2.8%m/m sa decline in December. The weakness in retail sales growth is partly due to the Lunar New Year seasonal effect. According to the HK C&SD, local consumer spending normally attains a seasonal high before the Festival. Historical data also suggests that retail sales tend to be weaker in January if the LNY starts in February. Thus, it would be more meaningful to examine the situation when both Jan and Feb data become available.

Figure 4: Hong Kong retail sales and private consumption
![](images/d284c2790f0e9233ecdaad812db2479ae51ff4b345d7b78d9e5bcd2d5d1824f3.jpg)

Looking ahead, we believe inbound tourism will still be the driving factor of consumption growth in HK, yet the imbalance between inbound and outbound visitor recovery may continue to weigh on HK<sup>’</sup>s consumption. In February, visitor arrivals from the Mainland averaged at 93.7% of the pre-pandemic level (2015-2018 average), while outbound HK residents averaged at 127.8% of the pre-pandemic level. On the other hand, the low unemployment environment, household income growth, together with various promotional campaigns and activities launched by the government, should continue to benefit the retail sector. In the near term, the LNY seasonal factor may have somewhat boosted retail sales growth in February. However, retail sales are not likely to get back to the pre-pandemic level soon.

## Taiwan: Export orders rebounded on tech

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export orders peaked in 1Q22 and trended down persistently through 1H23, reflecting the fading of the Covidrelated WFH tech demand boom, downturn in the global manufacturing sector, as well as de-stocking pressure in the global tech sector. From June 2023 onwards, the level of export orders appeared to have stabilized somewhat, followed by volatiliti in the past few months. In January, export orders showed a significant sequential rebound, rising 24.6% m/m sa, following the 13.9% drop in December. It was led by tech orders, which jumped 30.3% m/m sa in January, with a broadbased rebound in orders for electronics products as well information and communication products. Non-tech orders also showed a decent gain, rising 6.8% m/m sa in January (vs. -2.8% in December). By source of demand, the rebound was led by orders from US (+32.0% m/m sa) and the ASEAN region (+53.1%), along with a broad-based rebound in orders from China/ HKSAR (+12.1%), Europe (+10.6%) and Japan (+4.7%) (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Taiwan export orders by region (level) Index, 4Q19=100, sa, 3mma
![](images/f71e2122f0458349db501501b0680f898edf80a3e1c59cdce494eb11262fc496.jpg)

Going forward, we look for moderate growth in tech exports. That includes the structural boost for AI-related products, HPC-related demand, and likely improving consumer demand in the coming quarters. Meanwhile while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s destocking pressure has begun to ease. Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s tech export orders have underperformed actual tech exports since early 2023. This likely reflects the boost of AI-related tech demand (largely produced in Taiwan manufacturing plants), while Taiwan manufacturers<sup>’</sup> production lines in mainland China (orders for which are included in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s overall export orders compilation) seem to be restrained by regional supply chain adjustments. As such, the significant rebound in January export orders is encouraging, suggesting near-term solid performance in tech exports for Taiwan and possibly mainland China as well.

## January IP beat expectations

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s IP beat expectations in January, rising 16.0% overyear-ago. Seasonally-adjusted, we estimate that IP rebounded 5.7% m/m sa in January, reversing the decline of 4.5% m/m sa in December. The breakdown shows tech-related manufacturing IP rebounded 5.1% m/m sa in January, following the decline of 4.7% m/m sa in December. Non-tech manufacturing IP also showed solid gain of 5.0% m/m sa.

Regarding the major trends in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s export and industrial production cycles, merchandise exports returned to positive sequential growth starting from 2Q23, led by tech exports. This was followed by the sequential upturn in industrial production which began to come through since 3Q23, with the time lag between export and IP recovery reflecting the drag of inventory adjustment.

Following the aggressive inventory de-stocking by the Taiwan tech manufacturers since early 2023, manufacturing (tech) inventory has been turning up in recent months. It may hint at manufacturers<sup>’</sup> constructive outlook on near-term demand conditions. Tech inventory rose 3.3% m/m sa in December (or up 43.5% 3m/3m saar). The tech sector<sup>’</sup>s inventory to shipment ratio, having fallen consistently since early 2023, seems to turn back up again lately. Tech inventory to shipment ratio came in at 1.50, sa in December, compared to 1.36, sa in November (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Taiwan producer inventory and shipments Index, 2011=100, sa
![](images/cf3a6d7d4ebb33c8be8a3b5a6da460eb2fee760eea06d5f4839fe84f50145a64.jpg)
Source: MOEA, J.P. Morgan

## 4Q GDP shows moderate demand growth

Following solid 3Q GDP growth, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s economy continued to show strong growth in 4Q. Q GDP grew 4.9%oya (compared to 5.2% in the advanced 4Q GDP report). Seasonally adjusted, GDP grew 9.7% q/q saar in 4Q, following 3Q growth of 7.9% q/q saar. Details in 4Q GDP show moderate growth in exports of goods and services (4.2% q/q saar) and private consumption (2.6%), while capex remained sluggish (fixed capital formation contracted 6.4%). Upturn in the inventory cycle has provided some support to overall growth. For full-year 2023, Taiwan GDP grew 1.3%yoy.

Week of March 4 - 8

Merchandise trade (27 Feb)

On the domestic front, the recent easing in employment growth, and hence household income, will likely restrain the pace of domestic demand growth this year. Overall, we expect the Taiwan economy to show moderate growth going ahead, with average 2.2% q/q saar growth in 1H24 and 2.6% q/q saar in 2H24, reflecting moderate growth in industrial activity and domestic demand. Our forecast for 2024 GDP growth stays unchanged at 4.3%yoy.

## Manufacturing PMI eased modestly

After tracking a significant downward trend throughout 2022, Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing PMI readings appear to be on a rather volatile, moderate upward trend (from subdued level) since early 2023, with decent uptick through 4Q23 and going into early 2024. While the manufacturing PMI eased modestly in February, down 0.2-pt to 48.6, it followed the notable gain of 1.7-pt in January, and still registered the second highest reading in 12 months (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Global and Taiwan manufacturing PMIs
![](images/0fad4a0770585e25fdaad7aacc2ad0ed007143242df5f552736eb4217893bb14.jpg)

Regarding the cyclical momentum in the industrial sector, it is somewhat comforting to note that while the headline manufacturing PMI eased modestly in February, down 0.2-pt to 48.6, it followed the solid gain of 1.7-pt in January, and still registered the second highest reading in 12 months. On the cautious side, signals from the export orders component have been rather mixed, with a decline of 0.7-pt in February, following the 2.2-pt gain in January: this seems to suggest the encouraging, significant rebound in January export orders may reverse course in February.

In addition, we have noted the recent upturn in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inventory cycle as mentioned above, and here the recent trend in the manufacturing PMI<sup>’</sup>s inventory component seems to send a similar signal: the finished goods inventory indicator picked up 2.0-pt to 49.4 in February, highest reading since April 2022. As such, the manufacturing PMI order to inventory ratio fell to 0.96 in February, lowest reading in six months, which may somewhat restrain the room for industrial activity growth going forward.

## Korea

• Customs exports took a breather in February with lunar holiday

## • Next week: CPI 3.0%oya, IP -0.5%m/m sa

Customs exports fell in February, yet likely with under-controlled lunar holiday noise in the data. While the three-month trend growth is poised to slow, the exports<sup>’</sup> trend growth still holds up with tech sector<sup>’</sup>s strong impulse. In the longer-term perspective, the current account (CA) surplus was better than expected in 2023, and we revised up our forecast for 2024<sup>’</sup>s surplus to US\$57.1bn (BoK forecast US\$52.0bn) from US\$45.6bn. As hinted at by the gradual rise in the customs trade surplus in seasonally adjusted terms, we expect that the merchandise trade surplus recovery should lead the overall CA surplus thanks to reduced terms of trade drag and tech cycle recovery. That recovery should provide more room for the Bank of Korea to focus on the domestic policy environment, adjusting policy tightness if CPI inflation stabilizes near the target. We expect that CPI inflation will temporarily rise in February to 3.0%oya due to a base effect, yet will resume the stabilizing trend in coming months.

## Customs exports took a breather

Customs exports recorded US\$52.4bn in February, modestly above expectations. If seasonally adjusted (with conventional methods), exports fell 0.8%m/m, sa after rising 1.0% and 0.7% for the past two months, and three-month sequential growth decelerated to 6.9%3m/3m, saar from the 24.5% peak in December (Figure 1). That said, February<sup>’</sup>s monthly fall in exports is likely due to (at least partially) under-controlled lunar holiday noise. If the lunar holiday effect was controlled for, exports rose 3.9%m/m, sa in February after falling 3.7% in January, and the sequential growth was firmer at 17.2%3m/3m, saar.

Figure 1: Korea customs exports
![](images/893511ba122f6096f968fbf295aa352fab483e2c7e26995dd9228b56c6a3ca10.jpg)

In the product details, tech exports rose 2.4%m/m, sa for the seventh consecutive monthly gain, led by a 8.5% rise of semiconductor exports. Conversely, non-tech exports fell 2.1% for the second monthly fall as automobile (-13.4%) and oil products (-9.5%) exports fell. The divergence between strong tech (41.4%3m/3m, saar) and anemic non-tech (-4.6%) trend growth continued (Figure 2). Non-tech exports<sup>’</sup> February weakness is likely to be temporary amid lunar holiday noise. In the destination details, exports to China went through a correction, falling 5.9%m/m, sa after 6.2% growth in January, while exports to the US rose 2.1%, the sixth monthly gain during the past seven months.

Figure 2: Korea customs exports - tech vs. nontech
![](images/8b41cafb72c6a3b6aff69612beab7fffd7f88754db5d8c56f52e24f7936b891e.jpg)

Meanwhile, customs imports fell 0.5%m/m, sa for a fourth monthly drop, with the three-month trend at -10.1%3m/3m,saar. The fall in imports value likely reflects stabilizing energy import prices and cooling domestic demand pressure on imported goods. As a result, the customs trade balance in seasonally adjusted terms marked a US\$4.5bn surplus to record the eighth consecutive monthly surplus after the deficits from 2022 to 2Q23 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Trade balance - customs basis
![](images/3aa360eecce8e6c2a01ff6c57af5d5ee53a8c2093ae8324e3bc42a20c483f94f.jpg)

We continue to expect a positive contribution from net exports on 1Q<sup>’</sup>s real GDP growth, yet to a lesser degree than in 4Q23. The estimated and expected gap between exports volume and imports volume growth continues, yet to a lesser degree than before, as real export growth is likely to undershoot 2H23<sup>’</sup>s strong trend. While we need to confirm from next week<sup>’</sup>s January IP data, the slower real exports growth should imply a gradual slowdown in IP trend growth in 1Q. We expect modestly slower real GDP growth in 1Q24 at 1.8%q/q, saar vs 2.5% in 4Q23.

## ASEAN

• Philippines 2024 GDP forecast upgraded on resilient private consumption; capex outlook remains subdued

• Broad-based disinflation and Fed easing to pave the way for 100bps of rate cuts in 2H24

● BOP dynamics are favorable on narrowing CA deficit and robust financial inflows

We revise up our forecast for Philippines 2024 GDP from 4.2% to 5.3%, premised on resilient private consumption. However, the capex outlook remains subdued due to elevated borrowing costs and fiscal budget constraints. Broad-based disinflation is in process, with the recent Vietnam rice deal a potential catalyst to bring rice prices down. We continue to expect BSP to deliver 100bps of rate cuts in 2H24. BOP dynamics remain favorable as the CA deficit narrows and financial inflows stay robust.

## Cautious despite positive 4Q GDP surprise

4Q23 GDP expanded 5.6%oya and at an above-trend pace of 8.7%q/q, saar, beating expectations. The upside surprise was driven by private consumption (4.0%pt. contribution to headline growth) and fixed asset investment (2.2%pt.) even amidst declines in net trade (-1.7%pt.) and government consumption (-0.2%pt.). Full-year growth in 2023 came in at 5.6%, slowing down from 7.6% in 2022 and falling shy of the pre-pandemic average of 6-7%.

The strong 4Q23 GDP beat warrants a re-assessment of the cyclical outlook in 2024. In this regard, we have turned more constructive on household consumption and services. Labor market indicators (e.g., unemployment rate, labor participation ratio) continue to improve (Figure 1), while the drag from negative real incomes should subside as inflationary pressures abate. Nonetheless, the uneven recovery profile in household incomes may skew marginal spending towards non-discretionary categories.

Figure 1: Philippines labor market indicators
![](images/19f58ee6208154df12ea3c57243aadabc433420dba84e78882205d2fe4b91d65.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

However, we retain our cautious stance on the investment outlook. As mentioned earlier, elevated real borrowing costs are weighing on credit demand and loan formation. There are also scarce signs that the construction sector (over 60% of gross fixed capital formation) is bottoming out (Figure 2), especially in the residential property segment, given affordability issues, high vacancy rates, and soft demand.

Figure 2: Philippines residential construction permits vs. spending
![](images/eb0e355a47fce52594f5de3d8989dce2b6d4ec4cc09f27c3175c6144480d5ff3.jpg)

We also think that renewed optimism of a pick-up in public infrastructure spending is premature, if not unfounded. According to official sources (see link), the pace of completion of the Infrastructure Flagship Projects (worth PHP8.8 trillion or 36% of GDP) will moderate in 2024-26, with a significant portion of projects backloaded to beyond 2028 and still awaiting government approval (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Philippines IFPs target year of completion
![](images/5d1f0c16c71fa500997caa17f3f31f6bd5c02f82c1d3ab47f8e36d94dcfc20e3.jpg)
Source: NEDA, J.P. Morgan

In our view, this is a realistic assessment, as it likely considers persistent execution challenges and medium-term fiscal consolidation. Historically, the government has demonstrated a high degree of fiscal discipline, as shown in near 100% budget realization ratios (Figure 4), and notwithstanding stronger-than-expected tax revenues, public capex spending should increase by only about 5% per guided in 2024.

Figure 4: Philippines budget realization ratio
![](images/a189ee87802c039009505c958e610ba238a8bd4c02f0f863786bbf5ec46bdb71.jpg)

All things considered, we revise up our 2024 GDP estimate from 4.2% to 5.2%, mostly on base effects, but penciling in a sequential slowdown in 2H24 as global growth moderates.

## Disinflation and 100bps easing in the cards

Headline inflation moderated further to 2.8%oya in January (December: 3.9%oya), marking the second consecutive month that overall prices have returned to the 2%-4% target range. We continue to see broad-based disinflation across energy, food, and core categories (Figure 5) as global commodity prices ease and the lagged impact of previous FX depreciation subsides.

Figure 5: Philippines inflation components
![](images/1b1911ef1f91eff542daaaf7ed25fd6d571480e270c9d3b95632bbd1d00ecfb3.jpg)

As in previous months, rice inflation continues to accelerate (Figure 6) on higher import prices and the persistent lack of effective supply-side response. However, the recently penned rice deal with Vietnam could be the key catalyst to turn rice prices around. Vietnam has agreed to supply 1.5-2.0 million metric tons of rice annually in the next 5 years at a “competitive and affordable<sup>”</sup> price. This represents 50-66% of Philippines<sup>’</sup> annual import needs, and depending on the terms of trade, could incentivize private sector importers to release more supply to the market. We continue to expect headline inflation to moderate from 6% in 2023 to 3.4% in 2024.

Figure 6: Philippines rice prices
![](images/375bf8605da7b96368aff2c8b6f6f719ae12461987f7c9664f6386dfce8ca7f6.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Earlier this month, the BSP held the policy rate at 6.5%, as expected but highlighted upside risks to the inflation outlook despite revising lower their risk-adjusted CPI forecasts. Given uncertainties with regards to the inflation trajectory as well as the timing and magnitude of the Fed<sup>’</sup>s rate cuts, the BSP<sup>’</sup>s cautious tone is understandable. Nonetheless, we hold the view that a 100bps rate cut in 2H24 is on the cards, driven by disinflation and a Fed easing cycle starting mid-2024.

## Favorable BOP dynamics

December trade deficit narrowed significantly to US\$4.0 billion (November: US\$4.7 billion), driven primarily by imports compression in raw materials and mineral fuels. We continue to expect the current account (CA) deficit to narrow (2023F: 3.3% of GDP vs. 2024F: 1.6% of GDP) on stable, if not lower global commodity prices (Figure 7) and a subdued capex outlook (Figure 8).

Figure 7: Philippines raw material imports vs. CRB commodity index
![](images/0064456a4ac1e5a2150bb6b77ac1eea4efc473dc1f7e7afcd4f6d4e6ae138b08.jpg)

No data releases.

Consumer prices (Mar 1)
Figure 8: Philippines capital goods imports vs. public capex
![](images/aff169f4ab69d16028244033767772e65489555e603afae253e581fc91716461.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Despite narrower rate differentials and US dollar strength in the last two years, the financial account has posted a strong surplus, driven primarily by bank loans in the other investment balance (Figure 9). This has been another key pillar of support for the Philippines BOP since the pandemic and warrants further monitoring going forward.

Figure 9: Philippines other investment vs. policy rate differential US\$bn, 12-mo rolling sum
![](images/214e6e4caf1a57bbad027fce7928ac948fc5a2350f97d05a11ddf0543b8d596c.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Headline CPI posted an upside surprise on higher food prices but fiscal support measures and supply-side reforms over the years should keep inflationary pressures under control. We pencil in 50bps of rate cuts in 2H24, with the bias for more cuts if external conditions ease further.

## Malaysia

## India

• 4Q GDP prints much above expectation at 8.4% oya; GVA growth more subdued

• Fiscal year 2024 GDP growth expected at 7.6%

• Investments remain strong, helped by government capex

This week 4Q23 GDP growth far exceeded expectations, printing at 8.4% oya. GVA growth, which as we explain below may be a cleaner indication of underlying growth, was more subdued coming at 6.5%. The big upside surprise in 4Q GDP, alongside some upward revisions to the earlier two quarters, means that we expect Fiscal Year 24 GDP growth to be 7.6%. Within GDP, investment growth remains strong driven by government<sup>’</sup>s capex spend, while consumption growth continues to be soft.

## Strong 4Q print needs careful interpretation

India<sup>’</sup>s 4Q23 GDP (October-December) growth surged past expectations, coming in at 8.4% oya (J.P. Morgan: 6.5%; Consensus: 6.6%, RBI: 7%). While stronger growth is encouraging, the print needs careful interpretation . GDP is computed as the sum of GVA on the production side – the sum of value added across agriculture, industry and services and a Net Indirect Taxes (NIT) component, which comprises indirect taxes net of subsidies. As it turns out, GVA growth slowed last quarter to 6.5% oya – exactly as we had forecast – from an upwardly revised 8.1% oya in 2Q. Furthermore, implicit in the National Statistical Office (NSO) full fiscal year estimate is that GVA is pegged to slow further in the current quarter to 5.4% oya. The GVA trajectory is therefore reflecting dynamics that we have long expected (see, here and here): that growth in FY24 would be a tale of two halves – strong in the first half of the fiscal year followed by a discernible slowing in the second half of the year.

Figure 1: GDP and GVA growth
![](images/cd2cf4c20147d591eb131e0368b7df7cb919521d63b2854dcd7281b7615d4bef.jpg)
The reason, however, that these dynamics don<sup>’</sup>t show up in the quarterly GDP profile (see Figure 1) is because of the volatility imparted by the NIT component (Figure 2). NIT

growth surged to 32% oya in the October-December quarter, which is what boosted GDP growth to 8.4% even as GVA slowed to 6.5%. NIT dynamics are reflecting the timing of the subsidy payout within the year. Subsidies in the October-December quarter were more than 50% lower than the year before – when subsidies had surged on account of the Russia-Ukraine war – and that<sup>’</sup>s what pushed NIT growth higher. The extent to which this is contributing to headline GDP is visible in the fact that, with NIT growth expected to normalize in the current quarter, the NSO implicitly pegs GDP growth to slow to 5.9% oya in the January-March quarter.

Figure 2: GVA and Net Indirect Tax (NIT)
![](images/153e90ce66eac5225f3ca50ad7e65bd135e76fd0f4ab18353425247fa30fa19a.jpg)

The big upside surprise in 4Q GDP, alongside some upside revisions to the earlier two quarters, meant that our FY24 GDP growth forecast is now pegged at 7.6% . Furthermore, previous years also witnessed some revisions. FY22 was marked up from 9.1% to 9.7% but FY23 was marked down from 7.2% to 7%. The composite impact of these changes meant that the level of GDP was higher by 0.4%, post the revisions, entering FY24. After all the revisions, India<sup>’</sup>s growth trajectory from the pandemic now comprises a contraction in the pandemic year (-5.8%), then a rebound of 9.7%, followed by a slowing to 7% growth but then an unexpected acceleration to 7.6% this year, despite tighter monetary and fiscal policy and a slowing global economy.

## Industry outpaces services

The composition of the quarterly GVA had fewer surprises because, at 6.5% oya, it was what we had expected. Impacted by El Niño, agriculture predictably contracted 0.8% oya. Manufacturing growth was still strong at 11.6% oya but this was expected because it was boosted by strong earnings (\~14%) that are benefiting from lower input costs and the lack of double deflation, as we have discussed in the past (Figure 3). To put this in context, manufacturing IP growth last quarter had slowed to 5% oya and revenue growth slowed to 4% oya. However, with commodity prices stabilizing, the input cost tailwinds are transient and operating profits will eventually converge with revenue growth. In fact, that is already implicit in the January-March data wherein NSO implicitly pegs manufacturing growth to slow to 3.9% oya

from double-digit growth the previous two quarters. All told, industrial growth at 10.4% oya outpaced services growth of 7% oya. The one puzzle on the services side was strong public administration growth of 7.5% oya, even though central and state government spending was flat last quarter.

Figure 3: Private Manufacturing Firms Value Add vs IP
![](images/8c408898cc4db12c17bd634ffda6a7064ad2d702f0ec041c4e429345f3da5a0f.jpg)
Firms Value Add = Staff Cost + Operating Profits Source: RBI

## Strong investment driven by public capex

On the expenditure side, private consumption growth continues to remain soft, growing at just 3.5% oya. In fact, for the full-year NSO projects private consumption growth to be 3% which would be the lowest private consumption growth in 20 years outside of the pandemic year. Instead, growth was again driven by strong fixed capital formation growth of 10.6% (Figure 4) reflecting the capex push by both the Center and States this year and signs that real estate construction is picking up. On the trade front, with import growth outpacing exports, net trade subtracted 1.2 percentage points from headline GDP last quarter. Consequently, for a second consecutive quarter, discrepancies explain more than three percentage points of the 8%+ GDP on the expenditure side.

Figure 4: Private consumption and Investments
![](images/6f3d9fb02d25e9930b05982fea39d52a90ad172c36193636628cf5184c284e5a.jpg)

## Asia Focus: Where’s my tech capex?

Manufacturing production in EM Asia has seen a divergence between tech and non-tech, with the tech-related uplift most evident in Korea and Taiwan, which we expect will continue through 2Q24 (Figures 1 and 2). However, the tech sector makes up a relatively small portion of total manufacturing output, at 23% of the total. While its lift has been impressive, the more pedestrian performance of overall manufacturing is a reminder of that fact (Table 1). Indeed, the weakness in nontech manufacturing continues to weigh and has likely been a factor keeping regional capital spending lackluster through 4Q23, which we proxy via capital goods imports (Figure 3). While this is not surprising in the aggregate, the lack of capex momentum in Korea and Taiwan is a surprise (Figures 4 and 5).
